"I AM speaking," said a long-winded orator, "for the benefit of posterity." "Yes," said one of his hearers, "and if you keep on much longer your audience will be here." PAPA: "What did you think of the great bicycle race?" Little Son: "Didn't think much of it." "Everybody said it was wonderful." "I didn't see nothin' wonderful 'bout it. The one that winned couldn't help winning. He leaned over so far forward that he had to go like lightning to keep from fallin' on his nose."

Jesx ms KIND.—" We are getting up a concert for the benefit of the church. Won't you take a ticket?" Mrs. Sharpe: "My husband refuses to go to any place where there is to be music." "How lucky! This is just the kind of an enter- tainment he will enjoy." SIGNIFIcaNT.—First boy: "You're 'fraicl to fight, that's what." Second boy: "No, I ain't; but if I fight you, my mother'll lick me." goin' to your house." ees, however," said the distinguished foreigner, as he concluded his story, "simply a matter of hearsaid." "You mean hearsay, of

time ago !" "WHY, Clara, you look radiant. What has happened?" "I've just received an invitation to a wedding." "Well, there's nothing particular in that to go into raptures over." Ali! but it I.3.ppens to be my own." "You ought to name your dog gossip," said George to Maud's father; "I judge from an experience I had with him last night, that he is a good deal of a backbiter." OLD LADY: "Where is your husband, Mrs. De Long ? " Mrs. Pe Loud: "lie couldn't come •he has the rheumAtism all over him." Old Lady: "Goodness me ! and he's over six feet high !"

"How is i!," asked a lady, "that Time is always represented as bald-headed?" To which a gentleman replied, "So many have taken Time by the forelock, they probably pulled out all his hair."

MISS THIN: "Don't you think my new dress is just exquisite? They all say so." Fannie: "Oh, lovely! I think that dressmaker of yours could make a clothes-prop look gracefuL" "DON'T you get tired of answering foolish ques- tions ? '' inquired the lounger at the railway station. "Awfully," replied the booking clerk, with a dreary yawn. "I've answered that one more than fifty times this morning." HER FATHER (dubiously) : "I don't know what you are going to marry on. You have but a small salary, and my daughter has no fortune." Her Lover (confidently) : "Oh, well, if you are econo- mical for a year or two you'll be able to start us all right." Complainant: He hit me on the head with a hammer." HUSBAND: "Do you know that every time a woman gets angry she adds a new wrinkle to her face ?" MRS. GRAINGER (engaging new servant): "I am very particular about the conduct of my domestics. I had to discharge my last girl because I caught her winking at my husband." Applicant: "You did quite right, ma'am. A girl as careless as that ought to be discharged." A CURIOUS artifice of war was adopted by a Chinese junk when attacked •by a man-of-war. Tbe crew threw cocoanuts overboard into the sea, and then jumped in among them. Nearly all escaped, for it was impossible to tell which were heads and which were nuts.

To RUSSIA FOR WARETH.—Five years ago Lord Randolph Churchill undertook a journey to Russia. A friend asked him what he had gone there for. "To get warm!" was the reply. And he went on to explain the apparent paradox. "The winter is very cold, everywhere, but it is much colder in Russia than anywhere else. Hence the Russians take the most efficient precautions against its rigour. It is only in St. Petersburg that people understand the art of heating a house thoroughly." AN old lady from the country was at one of the • large London stations, when she noticed a carriage labelled "Engaged." It happened that in the said carriage were a young lady and a gentleman, who appeared to be on the best terms with one another. The old lady remarked that anyone could see they were engaged, without having the fact placarded to the world. A oxlvrizmaN in a restaurant got a fish-bone across his throat, and was very nearly choked. A . .

waiter rendered 'him a little 'assist ince by loosen- ing his collar and giving him a glass of water. When the gentleman received his bill he was dis- gusted to find that a shilling had been charged for this ,slight service. "What is the meaning of this, waiter?" he asked, angrily, pointing to the offending item. The waiter looked at it and re- plied: " Well, sir, choking's a hextra ! " "CHARLIE." remarked Fogg, "you are born to be a writer." "Ah I" replied Charlie, blushing slightly at.the compliment, "you have seen some of the things I have turned off?" "No," said Fogg; "I wasn't referring to what you had written; I was simply thinking what a splendid ear you had for carrying a pen. Iminen,s,e, Charlie; . . simply immense!" . Nor so long ago a well-known Canon of treat- minster was marrying two ,of his servants, one of whom was his footman. As had been his custom whenever he answered a question, the footman saluted. The Canon whispered to him to tell him to dispense with the salute, and simply say—after him. It was an unfortunate instruction, for When the question came, "Wilt thou have this woman to be thy wedded wife?" etc., the footman was obedient to the very letter, and said, "After you, sir."

A STRANG ER in London the other day, going to the City, asked a casual passer-by if he could direct him to New Broad-street. The fellow thought for a while, and then said he (lid not know, and proceeded on his way. Before the stranger had gone fifty yards farther he heard footsteps running in his direction, and looking back he perceived the man he had before ad- dressed, who, fearfully out of breath, rushed up to him and exclaimed : "Perhaps it is Old Broad. street you want?" "Well, where's that?" in- - quired the stranger, when to his surprise the fellow answered: "I don't know where that is, either."

It is difficult to conceive anything more dismal than the latest project for conveying human beings up Mont Blanc on a railway. There might be some possibility of a new and thrilling sensation in as- cending the monarch of mountains even in a car, if it kept outside and in the daylight. But surely the view from the summit would be dearly pur- chased at the cost of tunnelling right into the heart of the mountain and then being taken up a shaft by lifts. Nine million francs is spoken of as the cost of this wild scheme. Happily there is not much chance of capital being subscribed forso hair- brained a project. If we want to burrow in dark- ness there are plenty of struggling coalmines ,'Ftliat might be utilised for excursionists.

For a pretty specimen of Ulster intolerance com- mend us to the decision of the governors of Down Infirmary not to allow the local Sisters of Mercy to visit the Catholic inmates. Unfortunately,the•policy of these gentlemen is of a piece with that favoured in many of the workhouses and hospitals of Ulster.

The Conservative Press has clearly had its orders to he polite to Mr. Chamberlain. So the "Pall Mall " hastens to say that the only difference it has had with the member tor West Birmingham relates to London government (which Mr. Chamberlain has made a special pivot of his policy), and the " St. James's " protests that it loves him above all other men, bnt blames Mr. Balfour for nominating a Liberal Unionist in place of a Tory for Warwick. Only this and nothing more. And only hall-a- dozen vituperative articles from the " Standard," representing, with single accuracy, the inner feel- ing of four out of every five Conservative members of the House of Commons.

The House of Clmmons will miss Mr. Lawrence Gene. A simple, single-man, with a distinct oratorical gift born of many temperance lectures. Mr. Gane brought with him a certain air of good faith and native worth. His physique was frail enough. Guant and thin and white, he struggled for years with a feeble digestion and a failure of nervous power. A total abstainer, he was advised of late to try stimulants, and for some time the ex- periment succeeded. But this vital force was always low, and his rather premature death will not come as a surprise to his friends.

It is singular how little Frenchmen, even of the more intelligent sort, know about British public men. In a newly published French book on golf, it is casually stated that Mr. Leslie Balfour (now Mr. Leslie Balfour-Melville), the well-known Scot- tish golfer, is a son of Mr. A. ,J. Balfour.

There is a prospect that the Rev. Dr. Reuben Thomas, who is shortly coming on a visit to his native country, will settle here for the remainder of his life. The popular Congregational minister,who has made such a name for himself on the other side or the Atlantic. mil is, in fact, one of the finest preachers in Boston, is to occupy the pulpit of the church at West Hampstead in July.

The rector of St. John's, Horsley Down, has got into hot water with some of his parishioners for inviting the Rev, Father Walker, from St. Peter's. London Docks, to preach for him. Father Walker took the opportunity to advocate the confessional, Ind is said to have made use of these remarkable words, "The priest knows nothing what is told him in confession. He only knows it as God." I hear that the attention of the Bishop of Southwark, who is acting for the Bishop of Rochester, has been drawn to the matter, but as Father Walker is not engaged in the diocese,it is difficult to see what Dr Yeatman can do.

When commendatory note is made of the freedotn with which her Majesty goes about among the ~.)“)ple of Nice, divesting herself of the cumber- some formalities of State, the question naturally nrises why this favouring confidence is shown abroad and not at home. Here the Queen effectu- ally hides in public the more homely side of her character. She is never seen in London except from a distance, with a long perspntive of func- tionaries and armed guards fixed between her and even remote association with the populace. It can hardly he supposed that there would be less rever- ence for her as a Queen and an Empress if she occasionally showed herself to her own people, as she does to the foreigner, as human and a woman.

Somewhat belated contradiction is given to-day to gossip which transferred the new Speaker's paternity from the well-known Malvern physician to a turfite and prize-fighter who once represented Pontefract in the Comm ms. The story, which never "caught on," was only one of the many rumours that were industriously whispered about with sinioter design when Mr. Gully's name first began to be associated with the Speakership. There was, for instance, a wondrous fable, whose origin Was attributed to the region which rewirds itself specially charged with a mission to defend the faith. This related how there had been a con- v-rsion or perversion to Roman Catholicism in the Gully family in the last generation, with the effect that Mr. Gully, having been brought up as a , Catholic, bad lapsed into infidelity. But this canard never got start enough to make overtaking it. a difficulty. It was very promptly scotched, and I was never heard of again. And the other fiction gets chief prominence by its contradiction.

The Wartburg, which the Emperor William is ribnut to visit, as the guest of the Duke of Saxe- Weimar, is full of associations of a very different order from those cennected with hunting the caper- cailzie. It was in that rock-built fortress that Luther was sheltered by the friendly ccmpulsion of the Elector of Saxony from the foes who were hunting him. His ronmq, where he spent the time of his retreat in translittng the Bible, are still .shown, and the furniture which he used is piously preserved. There is something approaching to tlesecration in this association of the Wartburg. frinking down over the svvellino undulations of the 'Thuringian Foreet, with imperial expeditions for shooting eapercailzie.

The Admiralty has, I find. resolved on an import- ant step in connection with the vessels at present on foreign stations. During the next year no fewer than fifteen of those vessels are to he re-commis- sinned. It must not be imagined that this is due to any dearth of seamen, since the Admiralty has al- ready taken steps to relieve the time-expired men. It is, indeed, due to the belief that owing to the questions which have lately arisen it will be wiser that commanders should remain on the stations, who are acquainted with the local conditions and comprehend the views of the Foreign and Colonial Offices. No fewer than six of the fifteen vessels are commissioned to the China stations.

Mr. Campbell-Bannerman has protnised that the militia shell be supplied with a full-dress head- gear; and it has been asked whether this headgear will be of a uniform character, as,for instance, that discarded by the regularS. The reply is in the negative. Only one-fourth of the militia regiments will, however, get their supply this year.

I hear that the Liberals of Mid-Norfolk are in good heart and very hopeful of carrying Mr. Wilson's election. Meantime Mr. Clement Higgins is enjoying the panel fate of perverts—candid speaking from both sides. Having cut himself off from his old political associates to ally himself with the party whose boast is a monopoly of "the gentle- men of England," he finds himself not only pilloried by the publication of his lest election address, whose biting sarcasms tell chiefly against himself, but he is the recipient of uncomplimentary allusions by his new friends. The rector of Scarning,near East Drreham, is Dr. Jessopp, whose In/lounge is frank almost to the point of bluntness. When instructing the constituents as to their duty in the present Icrisis, the rev. gentleman remarked that. " they did not want a man like that ' wretched Tiller; nor a I Clement Higgins, who, because he could not get his I price, deserted the party who got him in by the ! back door. They wanted no pince-hunter or earl-et- bagger, no one who was with them to-day and gone to-morrow,but ROMP one'on whom:they could depend land who was well know to them all—one of them- selves, like their good friend Mr. Gurdnn." It mu-t be pleasant to Mr. Higgins to find himself thus ap- preeitypd by that party for whom he has sacrificed ISO much.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, APRIL O. 1895.

The common potato, when decomposing, gives light enough to read by. A Swiss statistician has taken the trouble to count the number of steps he`took in walkinq the whole ye4r. number he finds to have been 9,760,900.0 r an average of 25,740 steps a day. G,,ing still further into details, he declares that over 600,000 steps of these steps were taken in going up and down stairs. An instrument known as the" gastograph " has been constructed for the purpose of recording the motions in the stomach of a patient under treatment, the move- ments of the food while it is undergoing chemical action being carefully and minutely recorded by means of electricity. A single fly, if unmolested from the beginning of the fly season, will produce through itself and its successors in a single season, the almost unbelievable number of two millions eighty thousand three hundred and twenty flies.

Borax.

Borax is a very valuable article to key in houses, for it can be utilised to advantage in so many ways. It is excellent for softening water for domestic use in the laundry, kitchen, and bath-room, though it must not be used for softening drinking-water, or in cooking pur- poses; it may only he used in the kitchen for cleaning and for removing the fur or deposit which forms inside kettles, enamelled saucepans. &c. Borax is, too, one of the best means of rendering fabrics fireproof. It is good mixed with Keating's Powder to kill beetles. I have already referred to RS value in washing lace and fine things. It is also very useful te use in washing coloured things. It makes, too, a good hair wash, and may be used for cleaning teeth.

===== 0002601_18950420_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
Floor Cloth.

Floor cloths or oil-cloths should be dusted over every morning with a damp cloth. Once a week, net more at ten, they should be washed with a clean flannel kept specially for them with luke-warmaor neven cold water. Do not use any soap or soda. A little milk is a good thing to use occasionally. Ihey must be rubbed very dry when the washing process is over, in order to pre- serve their brightness. An oil-cloth should never be scrubbed with a brush ; it takes off the paint. Straw- matting should be washed but sP•ldom,as much dampness injures it. When it is necessary, however to clean a floor mat, do it by washing with a large coarse cloth dipped in salt and water, as you proceed wipiog it dry with another coarse cloth. The salt will prevent the matting from turning yellow. Cocoa-nut matting re- quiries taking up and shaking once a week. When very dirty it may be scrubbed with soap and hot water, then dipped in cold water and hung on a line to dry.

Punishing Children. Those who have not Lord Palmerston's happy belief that all children are born good are always prepared to admit that some provision for punishment is the neces- sary appurtenance of both domestic and school govern- ment, but the kind of punishment or the end and aim of punishment rarely gets the consideration it requires. The necessity for punishment is generally regarelf-d as a disagreeable accident which can be m,-t off-hand by such haphazard methods as chance to present themselves at the time. There are, however, five maxims worth re- membering in the control of the little people nature or chance commits to our care, which I believe originated with a German authority on this question. ti) Never correct a child in anger. (2) Never deprive a child of anything without returning it. (3) Never break a pro- mise. (4) Never overlook a fault. (5) In all things set before the child an example worthy of imitation. But parents,as a great philosopher says,are not good enough; they inherit so many faults and defects from their pro- genitors and their children in turn inherit so many from them that the Utopian home life which would be realised if these maxims were carried out becomes almost hop- less. The fact, however, does not prevent the attempt being made, for with each successful attempt evil quali- ties are nearer elimination. MEDICAL FRATTDS.-CAUTION.

No imposition can be meaner than foisting upon a sick man a worthless substitute for the medicine that will restore him to health. Newspaper reports of won- derful cures effected by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale PeoVe have led many to seek this remedy; but the public is IA arned against worthless pills sold loose, or by the dozen or hundred, and against imitation purporting to be "the same as. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills and cheaper." No substitute is cheap ; Dr. Williams' Pink Pills cure, while the others are of no effect. Dr. !Williams' Pink Pills are not like any other medicine, and no retailer can prepare them. They are only sold in wooden boxes, about two inches long, with directions, in a pink wrapper, printed in red, with the full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. If in doubt, seed the price (25. 9d.—or 135. 9d. for six boxes) to Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, 48, Holborn-viaduct, London. Dr. Williams' Pick Pills are a sure remedy for rheuma- tism, neuralgia, paralysis, locomotor ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, nervous headache, 'scrofula, chronic erysipelas, So. They are not a yurgative, but they restore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health, and are a specific for troubles peculiar to the female; while in men they effect a radical cure in all cases arising from worry, overwork, or excess of any nature.

SPEECH BY MR. GLADSTONE.

In St. DeinioEs Library at Flawarden on Monday afterneon Mr. Gladstone received a deputation repre- senting the federated Liberal clubs of Yorkshire, who presented to him a mark of appreciation of his life and work in the form of an oak bookcase and 160 volumes of books. Mr. Gladstone in returning thanks expresQed confidence in the future of the country. He said he hoped that the great body of the people, who had now been put in power, would show themselves to be proof againQt the temptations which invariably attended the possession and exercise of power The best way in which that could be done was by temperance and moderation, and, shove all, by preference of general and national interests to interests that were sectional. He thanked the deputation for their contribution to his library, and 9aid it was a delightful thing to see hew far and wide the institution of libraries was Pxtending.As competitors with the public-houses, and from other points of view. the value of litiraries could hardly be over-estimated. It was not surprising, perhaps, that light literature should receive so much attention from readers in libr a ries. He hoped, however, that there might always be a df.gire for good light literature, by which he meant works such as Scott, whom he regarded as a benefactor not only of this country but of the world at large. Earlier in the day Mr. Gladstone received in private a number of Armenian refugees, with whom he expressed his sympathy.

WE PARTED AT THE ALTAR;

BY LAURA JEAN LIBBEY, A'utla.). of "A Mad Betrothal," "lone," "Parted by Fate," " floribel s Lover," etc., etc.

CHAPTER XIX. A DESPAIRING GIRL. Tat whole affair had been so sudden, the shock which had followed Doris's startling revelation so terrible, that for an instant Frederick Thornton was dazed; and in that instant, the little figure had eluded his grasp, and with the fleetness of a deer, flew down the path that led to the river. " !" he called out, sharply. "Comeback 1 What wuld you do?" Only a heart-broken sob floated back to him. The words she had uttered came to his memory with awful horror:

"Only death could break the bonds that bind you to me, Mr. Thornton. I will not come between you and Vivian. lam going to set you free." Surely poor, beautiful little Doris did not intend to court death in the dark, rapid river. The thought brought him to hisdienses as nothing else could have done, and with fleet footsteps he fol- lowed that flying figure, calling upon her to stop. But if Doris heard those passionate cries she did not heed them. Pausing an instant on the brink of the moonlit water, she held up her white hands to the night-sky. "It was all a cruel mistake," she murmured, with gasping sobs. "He did not love me. It was Vivian whom lie loved. He hates me because I came between them and parted them, and I couldn't bear that. I must die and set him free. Surely. God must pardon me, knowing how bitterly hard my life is to bear." Poor Doris ! Her great sorrow had driven her mad with despair. With a piteous cry that pierced Frederick Thornton's heart like an arrow, as it floated back to him, little Doris flung herself from the rock on which she stood, down into ihe curling waves; and in an instant they closed over her head.

The next moment Frederick Thornton reached the spot. He stood there as if turned to stone, looking down into the rippling waters, sparkling under the pale light of the Moon—the waters that had but a moment before closed over that beauti- ful, dark head and childish, despairing face.

Horror had almost robbed him of his voice, deprived him of his strength. NVith an awful cry he staggered back from the brink, and rushed to- ward the house, crying loudly for help, for she had not risen again. Poor Doris, the fair young bride he had wedded, and from whom the hand of fate had parted, him, almost at the very altar, in so strange a manner, lying cold in death, and for his sake. She had not given him time to tell her how strangely fate had been playing at cross-purposes with them. She believed he had wilfully, cruelly deserted her, and it was this that had driven her to her doom. She had sought death ere the startling truth could be revealed to her. Oh, the pity, the horror, and the cruel shame of it I "Help! help !" he cried, hoarsely, wildly rush- ing up the broad, marble porch two steps at a time: To the servants, who rushed out in'answer to their young master's wild cries he explained in a few gasping words what he happened. There would be no question of saving her now, they told him. They could only get drags, and search for the body. This was quickly done. Steady hands bent to the oars. Rigid and still, and,ghastly white, Frederick Thornton sat in the boat beside them, watching it all; sat like a figure wrought in marble; sat with the ghastly, white, despairing face of a man whose thoughts were tor- turing him to madness. Every rush of the waves that beat against the boat every stroke of the oars, as they struck against the water, seemed to cry out to him: "She sought death because she could not live without you. How the poor child loved you I" The drags were dipped, splashed, and trailed through the shimmering water, only to come up tangled with weeds and river drift. No set, white, childish face, irane.d in rings of wet hair clinging to it—no slender, girlish figure came up with the drags from the river's depths. An hour passed in useless search. They were obliged at length to abandon it. The swift under-current must have carried her away before they had reached the spot. And all this time the merry ball was going on. Music, laughter and dancing made the night air echo with revelry, and not one among them knew of the tragedy which had taken place so near them. Frederick Thornton could not go back to the scene of festivity. He could not endure it. Like one mad, he paced the river's brink, gazing with horror and remorse too great for words at the fair, false smiling, treacherous waves. Suddenly a light, quick, step came hurriedly down the path. He knew before he raised him white, haggard, pain-drawn face that it was Vivian. "Have you learned what has happened, Vivian?" he asked. "Poor Doris has flung herself into the river. I was powerless to save her, for she never rose again. There was not even a ripphi to mark the spot where she went down. She is drowned. Poor pretty Doris i She did it to set me free. Oh, the pity of it, Vivian ; the pity of it 1" To the last day of his life Frederick Thornton never forgot the shock it gave him to hear the triumphant cry that broke from Vivian's red lips: "Dead, is she, Frederick? Was there ever such a fortunate stroke of fate for you and me? I thought I should go mad when 1 listened to her story and heard it confirmed by your lips. And now she is out of our path forever 1" Frederick Thornton recoiled from Vivian in horror too great for words. Could it be that he had heard aright? Great Heaven! had this girl, whom he had idolized as little less than an angel, a heart of marble, and a breast so dead to human pity that she could speak of poor Doris's untimely death like this? ' "Vivian I" he cried, sternly, "do you realise that you are speaking of that poor child's death ac though you were glad ?" "And so lam," assented Vivian Courtney. "Arc sot you, Frederick ?" "Heaven forbid I" he groaned, shudderingly. 41 If I could put life into that still heart again and brightness into those dim eyes, I would suffer a lifetime of vain to do it." Vivian came a step nearer to him and laid her hand—on which his engagement ring still sparkled —on his arm. Only a few short hours before that touch would have thrilled him to the heart's core. Now he shook it off with a shudder. "You have forgotten that, if she had lived, she would have come between you and me—and happi- ness," she murmured. And the voice that had always pounded like the sweetest music to Frederick Thornton's ears now seemed strangely discordant to him, and the vague thought drifted across his mind, was it possible lie had ever loved this girl? Heretofore he had seen only the sweet side of Vivian's nature. Now she stood revealed to him in quite a different light--a vindictive woman; one capable of the most desper- ate, relentless hate ; one who could glory in an inno- cent rival's death—the loss of a human life -if it removed an obstacle from her path. He was amazed—cruelly disappointed in Vivian Court- ney. The chances are, if Vivian had not given utter- , ance to her real sentiments, the whole course of ) her after life would have been different: A slight incident, a word, a look, have often been known to turn the mightiest love into abhorrence. Love comes to the heart swiftly, and it may take wing iist as swiftly, and is often but a transient guest. But, feeling so sure of Frederick's love, Vivian wen t on : "Yes, I am glad Doris has made away with her- self. How we would have hated her if she had lived to spoil our lives. She was always a design- ing, artful minx when she was at school. Of course she purposely remained until past the hour for the gates to be locked, to entrap you into mar- rying her—the crafty—" "Vivian !—Miss Courtney I.—remember you are speaking of the dead !" exclaimed Frederick Thorn- ton, sternly. " Do not speak another disparaging word of thas poor child if you would have me retain the respect in which I have always held you Remember—she was—my--wife." • Vivian took a step backward, and looked at his pale, angry face. "One would almost imagine that you were as much in love with the pretty little beggar as she was with you," she cried ; "and that you had just discovered that smouldering love existed in your heart when her uaexpected taking off awakened it into life." She had put the idea into his head, and he caught at the thought with strange eagerness. Was the great pain at his heart the quivering, mighty throb of love? He covered his face with his hands with a deep groan. The cmcdousness of the truth came home to

uu Lou r7onq Dora, tht• ,y. '1.1;1111ing young bride whom lie had we fro wi,om fate .ikad parted him so The ioity that had stirreri his pulses as he saw her k»eehog, with her golden head bowed, on the cold, 112 rd stones outside of the closed gates, crying out she would rather die than face the piti- less world—the mighty thrill that had stirred his pulses and bade him care for her—instead of pity, as he thought it then, was love. Love, too, that had prompted him to follow he to-night down to the river, to give his life, if need be, to save her. Love that bemoaned her loss, and cried out to him that his life was ruined and disolate, now that Doris was no more; and the bitterest drop in his cup of woe was the knowledge that Doris loved him so well, and that she had died because she could not live without him, and had died, too, believing he had wilfully deserted her. "Ah ! if she had but known just how that terrible affair came about."

Again Vivian broke in upon his perturbed thoughts, " You do not speak! You do not attempt to deny it !" she cried, shrilly. " I believe you did love the girl, or you would never, never have asked her to marry you that night of the ball; and if that be true, I glory in the fact that you are parted from her—that she is dead I"

Great Heaven! how the cruel words smote him ! and in that moment he loathed Vivian more intensely than he had ever loved her. "As the case now stands, Vivian," .he said, coldly, "Ii release you from your engagement, realising under what circumstances it was made." "But, supposing I do not wish to be released," she said, slowly. " We can never be anything to each other in the future," he answered, firmly. " All is over between us, Vivian; we must part forever."

CHAPTER XX. A HUSBAND'S FAULT.

Mr& Thornton's dismay upon hearing the start- ling story Vivian related so incoherently to her can better be imagined than described. She sought out her son at once. He was still pacing to and fro under the beeches in the garden. "Frederick," she whispered, approaching him and taking his cold hands in her own, "you must not give way to grief like this. Under the circum- stances, you are blameless. That accident, and the delirous fever which followed it, completely oblit- erated all knowledge of your bride from your mind. The fault lay with Doris herself. When she came here, why did she not tell me all, instead of creeping into the' house by stealth, and in dis- guise? Or why did she not confront you, and demand to know why you deserted her? Then matters could have been righted."

"She was so young, mother, she did not know what to do under the complicated circumstances. She believed, of course, that I was false to her," he groaned. "There is another matter of which I wish to speak, Frederick," said his mother, timidly. "This is scarcely the time and place, still it must be said, sooner or later, and the subject may as well he gotten over with first as last. "You married this girl on the spur of the moment, my boy, and even at the time you did not love her—you loved Vivian. Believing yourself after- ward free to woo and win Vivian, you became engaged in marriage to her. The wedding day is Set; she wears your ring upon her finger. You cannot bring Doris back • therefore it is best to keep all knowledge of this tragedy from the public, and let your marriage with Vivian go on." She was puzzled by the strange look that crossed his face, leaving it paler than ever.

"I will keep this pitiful aflair from the world's knowledge for my poor, unhappy Doris's sake, if you think best mother," he answered, huskily; but as for marrying Vivian now, mother, I can- not. Do not urge me. I cannot." Mrs. Thorntor looked into her son's white, handsome face wan dismay, not unmixed with with fear. Was this terrible affair driving him mad? Was he losing his reason? He was evincing an unaccountable, strange dislike to- ward Vivian whom he had fairly idolisdd. Surely this was a sign of it. Those struggling on the verge of madness usually turn against the one, they love best—first. "Do not talk about it any more, mother," he went on, huskily. •` I cannot hear it." Every effort was made to find poor Doris's body, but of no avail. If she lay beneath the water, the waves kept their secret well. • The disappearance of Mrs. Thornton's companion created no excitement whatever. No one missed her, no one took any interest to know what had become of her; and thus days lengthened into weary months. At last society was beginning to wonder why the marriage between Vivian Courtney, the heiress, and Frederick Thornton, the banker's son, did not take place. Since the night of the ball many had noticed a sudden and growing coldness between the lovers. No one knew that, seeing Vivian in her true light—on that fatal night she had boldly declared her hatred of poor Doris—the last remnant of Frederick Thornton's love for her had been turned into loathing. He no longer sought Vivian's society ; he rather avoided it. Whole hours he spent by the deep, dark, treacherous water, his face buried in his I hands, groaning aloud. It was too late now for vain regrets. His heart had gone out to poor Doris too late. No one knew how he mourned in secret for the bride he had lost so strangely. To Vivian Courtney this strange revulsion on "the part of her lover was maddening. In vain she tried to woo him back to his allegiance; but the golden spell of love was broken. He shrank from her. The dead coals of the old love were neve& astean to be re-kindled in his heart. ; Yet Vivian Courtney would not be discouraged. Even to herself she would not admit the alarming truth that Frederick Thornton's heart had grown cold to her. One afternoon matters came to a crisis. Vivian drove up to the villa and called for Frederick. "I should like you to ride a little way with me, Frederick," she said. "I have something to say I to you." Very reluctantly he complied, rather dreading a i tete-a-tete with the wilful beauty, and a shade of .1 annoyance crossed his face which she was not slow in perceiving. For some distance they rode in silence—a silence broken only by the twittering of the birds in the trees overhead, or the drowsy hum of the bees amid the sweet pink clover. At length the silence became irksome to Vivian. She clutched the reins tightly with her ungloved hands; and looking down he noticed that she still wore his engaeernent ring. She saw the glance, and a deep flush, followed by a quick pallor, spread over her face. "Frederick," she said, in a low voice, "it is about this ring I wish to speak to you. Do you still mean what you said on the night of the ball ?" It hurt her pride cruelly to utter those words, but she most know. Her love—her whole future— was at stake. He tried to overcome his embarrassment at the question, hut it was a failure. He looked greatly distressed. How could he tell her that his heart had changed toward her since the evening she had expressed herself so freely in regard to poor Doris's death? How he hated the ungracious words he must speak : yet it must be done. He must speak plainly to her. The words he must utter : "I have ceased to love you," seemed to him most unmanly : yet the sooner they understood each other the better it would be for them both. in the gentlest words he could command, he told her that the engagement they had entered into should never have existed, owing to the fact that, at that time, he was not free to woo and win any young girl's heart, and he begged her to remember the accident which had befallen him, followed by brain fever, which had obliterated from his mind all knowledge of his bride, who awaited his return. .

"No, no, Vivian," he went on, "do not con- sider your promise binding, for I do not. You are as free as air." The words died away on his lips as he saw the marble white caller of the girl's face. Her eyes glowed like purple fires, and her breast heaved con- vulsively. "You have no heart, or you would not break with me ! " she sobbed. "All the world will know that I am a victim to the fickleness of a ma's love!" she cried. WHAT AILED THE FIRE?

rh° Cools really weather of autumn was come. After sunset t he Was a frosty feel in the air. "We had better make up a little fire in the grate to-night," said nIY wife ; "it's chilly sitting here without." " Yes," said I, "Let us make the fire now." So I went at it Putting on plenty of dry wood and coals enough to light it uP nicely. But it was no use—the fire wouldn't burn. It would just spit and sputter and make a smudge fit to Pot your eyes out. We gave it up and went to bed early. Next morning I investigated and found the chimney neatly closed with swallows' nests. The wood was tight, the coal was right, but the draught wasn't. I evicted the swallows,and cleared away their halatations, Result : Next night a good fire and pure air. Dear me! Stich a simple thing is hardly worth telling, is it. Perhaps not, but here is our friend, Mr. Joseph Smith, Neho talks like this: " I had no relish for my meals," he time NOW, why not? BY MR. U. (Continued.)

Other matters are, as I have said, in the previous article, private ownership of land, private owner- ship of all the means of transportation and all such matters as come daily within the ken of us all. It must be clear to all that the life we now lead is a highly artificial one. Instead of diminishing, our daily wants are steadily increasing, and yet the tendency is to make cheaper, and yet cheaper every thing that is brought into the house. 1, and others, are constantly complaining of the evils of competi- tion. Do we understand it? Do we understand the money question? Not a bit of It. Competition it is, I suppose, that enables me to buy flour at 90 cents a sack of 501bs. Three years ago for the same weight I gave a dollar and 40 cents. Sirloin of beef is 4d. a lb., and a leg of mutton is bought for the same money. 'three years ago this meat would have cost from 81. to 93. a lb. A fair pair of boots can be had for Bs., but a good suit of clothes will cost ..£B, and a hard felt hat 103. If we look into these figures a little we see very cogent reasons why the Socialist programme should be adopted. We in America produce more flour than we require, and home competition, where it can have full and fair play, keeps it down in price. Our surplus you in England get, and the Argentina, Russian and legyptian flour competes with it, forming a!formidable barrier against which the British farmer cannot hope to prevail, under pre- sent conditions. We now feed the pigs with wheat, because we find that wheat pays best when changed into the unclean swine. When fishermen catch too much fish, they throw it overboard rather than glut the market and so bring prices down. When nature gives us a bountiful harvest the far- mers put their heads together and wisely conclude that everything is going to the dogs, and the amount of acreage under the plough must be re- duced. The amount of fruit now grown in Cali- fornia and the quantity of wine pressed is simply ruining the State, and the wine presser and the fruit grower are in the ridiculous position of not daring to lower the pro- duction, lest it remain as it is, or increase it. Every shoe store, and every clothing store is crammed to the ceiling with goods, and every tradesman is anxious to spend heaps of money in advertising the public that he is selling his goods below cost price. " Ruinous sales," and " must be sold because we want cash," are advertisements that meet one at every turn in an American city. A simple minded man might think these tradesmen were lunatics to so advertise their ruin, but the better the business man the more advertising. So it appears that the more corn we grow the poorer we get. The more shoes we make, the more bare feet thereare around, the more clothes and the more rags. Don't laugh at the folly of all this, and question its truth, because you know that when stocks get large in your forges things are looking bad, and that when the coal heaps become too high around the pit's mouth either the miner or his employer will take about limiting the output. Generally the employer adopts the correct business method of offering a cut in wages, this usually brings about the desired strike, and then the employer can piously raise his selling prices, and so7make money out of his Christian brothers' adversity. What a mad world this is. Now if we only manufactured what we really wanted, and every body did a little towards supplying his own wants, and those of the infirm and aged, and no one was seeking his own personal profit. What a happy world this would be. I say again, don't laugh because Utopian though it may appear, some of us have read in -an old book about a good time that was to come when the sword should be beaten into the plough share, and the lion should lie down with the lamb. Perhaps this was all a fable; who knows? The only people have ever met with who denied point blank the adaptability of the ethics of Jesus Christ to the every day life of this world were people who called themselves Christians, and seemed in a mighty hurry to call me infidel. This sort of people per- secuted Mr. Bradlaugh to the death, and then he- slavered his memory with their meaningless praises. The past few years have been pretty hard ones for the American working men. Large armies, made up of the unemployed,have been marshalled and led on to Washington, there to be sold by officialdom that they were not wanted, and must disperse, or the gaol doors would be opened to them. In War- rington the unemployed have been led to the Town Hall there to be met again by officialdom and told to go about their business, while their leader was told something very much more pointed. One would think that it was quite sufficient if Mr. Price was an Englishman to justify him in speaking to, and leading a body of Englishmen. If we could look at this question equarely,it seems to me that we ought to be thoroughly ashamed of ourselves for our utter childishness. It is right for a town to invite a stranger to represent it in Parliament, but it is not right for the poor ignorant workman to invite a stranger to lead them to the Town Hall, strange, very strange. Men are our most abject slaves on election days ; when in office they are our masters. Previous to election they are willing to kiss all our babies; they are even willing to turn our wangles. hut hen elected, we must not even go to our own Town Hall, and plead for work, or bread for our little ones. Are all these crowds of unemployed. thriftless, drunken half demented creatures? If so, what, and who has made them so? Are they, or are they not the outcome of vile social conditions that existed before they were born? Before litera- ture and education were made popnlar, we can understand why might should be right. Now the case is altered, tbe man who is right should have might en his side, and the man who insists in Ipreaching from the housetops the fact that the old order has changed and must give place to the new ! and better, is the man who has right on his side and the people must aid him with their might. All

THE E KAIVIINEP, sAT TJPDA y, APRIL 20, 1895.

officialdom is, or should be the servant of th+ people. Thi, is a doctrine that should appeal to neither class or plejudice. but to sound common sense, and a man who has hitherto prided himself on being a Tory and who now finds himself a Conservative Socialist Would have me for a fellow-worker, but in a different camp. The fact that stands out. in bold- est relief to-day is the one that tells us that no class of men, no body of men is satisfied with things as they are. The manufacturer and the workmen are feeling their way to more amicable relationship, as witnessed to in your own Chamber of Commerce debates recently. The landowner and the tenant would hail with joy any practical scheme that would give them more liberty and thus make the land of some use. The rich speculating capitalist would sleep with more comfort if lie ' could do so without dreaming of electrical contriv- ances for catching burglars, or that when he died his body would not be kidnapped, or that, while living his children would not be stolen. Surely the unrest is all around us,then why so much objection to what I have before called experimental Social- ism. It is now too late to ignore or oppose Social- i,in as an idea. Our industrial order has reached its last form of life and expression. Among the masses it has degenerated out of old-time neigh- bourly competitive rivalry into the questions as where the next job and meal are to come from, or who are to be the next victims of commercial can- nibalism. Among capitalists it has evoluted into the " combine," where all individual conscience and responsibility are stifled, if not altogether ignored, while greed and selfishness are abnormally developed and stimulated in the pursuit of unlaw- ful spoils, and dividends on watered stocks. Keir liardi's Socialism would take the unem- ployed on a voyage of pillage. The evolutionary Socialist, or if Mr. Bennett likes it better, the Con- servative Socialist would give his whole thought and intelligence to construction, seeking the pos- sible, and so, by experience making the experimen- tal of today the sure foundation of the construc- tion of to-morrow. One of the two ways will cer- tainly be adopted before we are much older; it re- mains for the thoughtful intelligence of the world to say which shall it be. I am somewhat of opinion that we look at this question from too narrow a standpoint. While the rich man will scheme and try all he knows to keep his wealth, the poor man will certainly try as hard to keep as:far away as possible from the mere subsistence point. The poor might reasonably be . invited to look outside of themselves, and see what mother nature was doing for them. If it is found that the forces of nature are at work in a thousand different ways grinding out slowly yet surely material for our use, why should we not seek through the ballot box, to make certain things un- lawful and lawful, and so make Socialism the law of the land step by step. It should be unlawful for the individual to own land and its wealth of bottled sunshine. Mr. Bennett and I would at once vote for its confiscation and make it lawful only for the State to own land. Confiscation would be our motto, and our aim would be the commonweal. The inventor, would find himself displacing the mere man of ancient lineage, and he who discovered a new force in nature would realise the fact that a new aristocracy was growing up of which he was a shining light. Such a man as Edison would cease to be the mere tool of a "com- bine," and would know that his name had indeed become a household word. Do Socialists dream? I think not. The discovery of the " Oscillator," by Tesla will reduce to old bell metal half the machi- nery at present moving on the face of the globe. The discovery of the electro magnet will clear out the hated iron clad, while the adoption of the type setting machine will make cheap literature possible without charity's aid. 807, Page-st., San Francisco.

NARROW ESCAPE OF THE RESIDENT ENGINEER. In consequence of the successful removing of the dam- aged gates, the Hispania, belonging to Messrs. Henderson Bros., and the Keernum, of the China Mutual Steam Navigation Company, which had been lying at Eastham were notified that the Canal was clear,and the Hispania, which was followed later on by the Beemum, entered the lock about half-past three in the afternoon of Monday. She had come slowly up the Canal in charge of two tugs, the pilot of the owners, as well as the Canal superin- tendentsl along the various sections being also on board. The Hispania did not proceed beyond Latchford. where she remained all night. Precautionary soundings were taken immediately before the Hispania arrived at Latchford,and these proving satisfactory the lower gates were opened,and the vessel came leisurely through. The Hispania having cleared,one dreaded result of the recent accident vanished, and it was seen that with two gates the lock could be satisfactorily worked and vessels carried over the top of the wrecked gates. The raising of the gates is, however, a matter of no small difficulty, and it is expected that before they can be got out the water in the lock will have to be lowered to more than its normal extent. A ceetrifugal pump has been erected to pump the big lock out if found necessary. When raised the gates will be carried to a point opposite the old shed at Thelwall, where they were constructed. There they will be repaired. A startling, and unpleasant incident to one gentle- man occurred during the operations of the Ranger. The resident engineer, Mr. Congreve, who has been exceed- ingly energetic in connection with the salvage opera- tions, was in the act of stepping across a plank which connected the Ranger with the quay, when the ship sheered off a little, and, the plank slipping, Mr. Con- greve and the plank were together precipitated into the water. Mr. Congreve went clean out of sight, and some sensation was of course caused on the Ranger and on the canal banks by his sudden immersion. It was not long before he reappeaeed above the water,and then he struck out bravely for the iron ladder running up the lock wall. He fortunately gripped this at once and clambered out nimbly. Beyond a pod drenching and a slight shock to the system, the resident engineer was apparently none the worse for his adventure.

THE RIGHTS OF POST-OFFICE

SERVANTS.

Mr. Arnold Morley, the Postmaster General, having been asked by Mr. J. A. Murray Macdonald, M.P., what are the rights of the servants of the Post-office with regard to combination, has sent to Mr. Macdonald a copy of a letter he recently addressed to a correspond- ent who had asked a similar question. In that letter he affirms that all the privileges whichtrade unionists enjoy are acaorded to the unions of postal officials. What, as the head of the Department, he is linable to concede is something quite different. Re has always been open to receive deputations, but be has declined, and will continue to decline, to allow them to be accompanied either by members of Parliament or by other persons not actually in the service of the Post-office. In this he I has followed a well-understood rule of the Civil Service, hut he is anxious as far as possible to afford all classes of employes the fullest opportunity of approaching him.

A WEIRD STORY,

It is a remarkable story which Mr. Wells is contribut- ing to the pages of the 'New Review." Mr. Wells has hit upon a very striking conception, and one of gruesome horror. Projecting himself over a thousand years by the aid of his convenient Time Machine, he arrives on the site of London in the twenty-ninth century. He finds himself among an amiable, refined, and delicate race of men, who have lost all the virile virtues.and are haunted by what is to him at first an inexplicable fear ofthe dark. After a time he discovers that these graceful creatures who dwell in the light and sunshine on the world's sur- face constitute one section of the human race, represent- ing the ultimate product of a superfine civtlisation which had driven the proletariat underground. There the workers had increased and multiplied, accustoming themselves to the darkness. They emerged from their subterranean haunts in the darkness of the night and carried off the helpless and unresisting aristocrats of the surface to supply their larder! The future, according to this fancy of Mr. Wells. will belong to the cannibals, who will preserve the men of the upper world as men now preserve pheasants for the table.

SIIIP CANAL.

LATCHFORD LOCK GAT 4 SNI ASH ED.

The first accident in connection with the Latch- ford Locks occurred under serious circumstances on Thursday afternoon last week about five o'clock. An eve-witne—; states that the steamship " Harold," a medium sized boat, owned by Messrs. Edwards and Robe,tson, Manchester, and outward bound from Manchester was signalled to enter the 450 ft. lock—the size of the large lock when the inter-mediate gates are closed. -Instead of slowing down, however, the vessel apparently came full speed ahead, and passing through the open outer gates she crashed her bows on to the gates at the closed end. The impact was terrific,and the right hand gate was torn completely from its hinges and hurled into the water, where it sank at the entrance to the lock from the Liverpool end The remaining gate was forced partly from its fas- tenings, and hung half-submerged by the roller chains from the lock-wall. The vessel, of course, after passing the gates had a sudden drop of Ifift. to the lower level of water on the Warrington side of the locks. This, with the immense volume of water behind, -.mused her to plunge deeply into the seething turmoil below. The force of the blow made an enormous crash, while the water rose and half covered the'quivering "Harold," whose crew 90 suddenly awakened to their danger, shrieked aloud as the vessel went down. Fortu- nately, after the collision the boat righted herself, but was dashed against the wall before she could g(-t free. Meanwhile,the officials seeing the impend- ing calamity and the impossibility of averting it, put on the engines to close the top gates and stop the rush of water. The result was that the gates instead of coming steadily and meeting at a safe angle were separated, and one, the left hand one, went in front of the.other, so that the latter came directly behind it and with a resounding crash drove it forward, so that the immense structure is bent into a curve while the water still rushes through the uneven joining and strained hinges. After leaving the lock,the "Harold," whose bow-plates near the water line were stove in, rounded the corner and was moored to the bank in the sluice-bay. Here the water in the ballast tank was released to lighten her, and says the on- looker, it poured in a steady stream through the fissures in the plate. No one on board was injured but a small fiat working at the bank close by was so violently upheaved hy the sudden rush of water that a workman was thrown off into the canal. He was immediately recovered, however. Later on in the evening the position of affairs was critical from several reasons. At the top of the lock the gates were PO jammed that a body of water about 10ft, long was pouring through the fissure with threatening violence. Inside the lopk the water was greatly agitated and appeared likely to displace the remaining intermediate gate from its broken hinges. Each gate is of ponderous green heart, weighing 300 tons, and the one sunk prevented the outer gates being closed to relieve the pressure. Looked at calmly the gate presented a very wrecked appearance. Its top platform of 3m, planks was split across, while the sheets of iron at the butt were twisted and broken in all directions. On the opposite wall where only the socket of the other gate was left,there was not much to see but what there was, was very signific- ant. The bolts with which the structure was originally supported, though 3i inches thick were snapped asunder like the proverbial carrot. Accord- ingly, there was nothing between the higher and lower reaches of the canal but the damaged lock gates. To all appearance the gate was gradually giving way to the pressure, and little wonder the officials worked with might and main to stave off the impending disaster. Needless to say, if the gate gave way before the lower gate was closed, the re- sult would be disastrous, and it would practically have involved the draining of the upper waters of the' canal from lrlam locks, and in such a manner that the immense rush would certainly very greatly damage the canal as well as any vessel which might be between Irlam and Latchford. As it is,until the lock gates have been repaired, larger vessels will be prevented from using the canal, as they have to make use of the large lock, and it is of course use- less in its present state. Large numbers of people visited the lock as soon as the nature of the acci- dent became known.

Mr. Hunter, the chief engineer of the canal, was soon on the spot, and arrangements made for rais- ing the sunken gate. A diver was sent down and the position of the impediment examined. Consequent on the large lock being blocked by the gate,all the vessels small enough were tompelled to use the lesser lock. Some apprehension was felt as to the larger vessels which were expected. It would be impossible to let them through and the conse- quent delay would entail great expense. Strenu- ous endeavours were made to effect a clearance,but up to Wednesday night nothing material bad been effected.

On Thursday the officials were unable to affect any clearance of the broken gates though the tug "Gladiator "and a huge flat with a powerful winch mounted upon it has been at work. We understand that the driver have been down and attached chains to the sunken gate, but the machinery at present available was not powerful enough to remove it. No attempt has yet been made to place the top gates in proper position, that being impossib.e, but pre- cautions has been taken in the shape of ponderous chains and ropes rove round the mooring stumps fixed on the lock wall and from them to the over- taxed gates. To create further confusion the traffic on the Canal has been great since the disaster, so that the remaining lock has been kept constantly occupied letting the vessels through. Our represen- tative was informed on good authority that there, is every likelihood of all the vessels, big and little at present in Manchester being accommodated in the centre lock, so that the doubt existing in the minds of many will be dissipated.

REPORT OF THE CHIEF ENGINEER.-SERICUS

ALLEGATION.

The following is the official report of the Company's engineer. It is addressed to the Directors : Engineer's Office, Manchester, 13th April, 1895. My Lord and Gentlemen,—At about 5.10 p.m. on Wed- nesday, the 10th instant, the s.s. Harold, of Glasgow, belonging to Messrs. Colville and Co., of that city, after having called at Partington to load bunker coal, entered the 650 ft. lock at Latchford at a speed estimated at eight miles per hour. The lock was all ready for the reception of thE vessel, the upper gates being open, and the lock- master and piermen were all at their posts waiting for her. The speed at which the Harold was approaching the lock had been observed by the lockinaster, who repeatedly hailed the ship, instructing the captain to go full speed astern. The captain appeared then to be making attempts to communicate with his engineer but apparently without success, as the vessel rushed through the lock at the same speed at which she had entered it, crashed through the intermediate gates, leaped the drop of 16ft. from the Latchford pond to the estuary level, and watkonly brought up at a short dis- tance above the railway viaduct. The engineer of the company, having spent the day in an inspection of the works of the estuary section, had just reached Latchford in the Florrie, which was then about to be locked up from the lower to the higher level ; the engineer was, therefore, upon the ground when the accident occurred, though unable to actually witness it from the position of the Florrie in the adjoining lock. Accompanied by Mr. Congreve and Captain Waring, the engineer boarded the Harold at once, and informed the I captain that the vessel (which was but little damaged) was not to be moved, but was to be tied up in the sluice way, as directed by him. The captain of the Harold was unquestionably under the- influence of drink, as were several others amongst the party he had on boatd with him. Amongst this party were Captain Dudley, the superintendent of the canal from Thelwall to Irlam, and Mr. Soratt, the berthing master at Partington. Of the intermediate gates the northerly leaf was car- ried completely away and deposited upon the gate plat- form of the lower gates, thus preventing them from being worked ; the southerly leaf, though not carried away, was torn from its fastenings and completely I wrecked. When Mr. Marshall, the lockmaster, saw that I a collision between the Harold and the intermediate I gates was inevitable, he at once attempted to close the upper gates, hoping to be able to get them together be- fore the complete destruction of the intermediate gates, and thus save the drawing off of the water from the Latchford pond. The extraordinary rapidity, however, with which the Harold broke through the intermediate gate prevented the upper gates from being closed in time, with the result that the rush of water caught the upper gates and drew them forward with such velocity that the southerly leaf was carried ahead of the norther- ly to an extent of at least five feet at the upper end of the mitrepost and the leaf strained in such a manner as to leave very little hope of its ultimate preservation. Preventive tackles were fixed to the upper gates, and I fortunately, although so severely strained, the gates held and supported the pressure eue to the head of water upon them. All the plant available for the purpose was immediately brought unon the ground, and several attempts were made to lift the sunken leaf of the inter- mediate gates, so as to enable the lower gates to be closed. These attempts were not successful, so the manager communicated with the Liverpool Salvage Company, with the result that the powerful salvage steamer Ranger was brought into the lock on Thursday evening, and after some difficulty succeeded on Friday, the 12th instant,in moving the gates sufficiently to allow the lower gates to be closed. When this was done and the water in the lock raised to the higher level it was found that the upper gates could be readily worked, and that after the collar-straps, Arc., had been readju.ted the gates had come back to thelr original shape in a way which could hardly have been expected, and although the gates were , damaged they were still in a condition which would ad- mit of their being used. This has removed the fear of a lengthened stoppage of the large lock at Latchford, and the efforts of the Ranger are now being directed to the removal of the two leaves of the wrecked gate, so that the lock may be available for traffic. The leaves of this gate will he placed upon the slope of the south side of the canal, abreast of the old gate shed which is still standing at Thel wall. The slipway is being constructed at that place, so that the gate may be hauled eut of the water and, if possible, repaired so as to be serviceable again. The Harold is still detainPd at Latchford under

the powers of a writ of attachment which has been ob- tained from the Admiralty Court. It is hoped it will be possible to lock the Hispania which is now in the canal at Eastham, through the large lock at Latchford on Monday next, which is a condition of things for which the most sanguine of per- sons could not have ventured to hope on Thursday list. I am, &c., • _ .

W. FIENRIr HUNTER, Engineer. STEAMER SUNK AT RUNCORN.

Early on Good Friday morning a collision occurred near the docks at Runcorn between the steamers Tele- graphic, going seawards, and the Hannah Beckett, of Liverpool, which was crossing the waterway from the Mersey to the Runewrn Docks. The cause is attributed to a misunderstanding as to the cods of whistles. For- tunately,theoutward-bound steamship was going slowly. As It was, however, she struck the Hannah Beckett amidships,and so smashed her bulwarks that she rapidly began to fill with water. The crew, consisting of H. Ainscow, of Cawder-st.,captain ; Thomas Potter, Balfour- et., engineer ; Thomas Houghton, Cambridge-st., fire- man ; and J. Wilson, Portland-A., mate, all of Runcorn, received prompt assistance from those belonging to the Telegraphic,and by means Of ropes were drawn on board that vessel. The injured vessel sank within two minutes of the collision. It is fortunate that she did not go down in mid-channel, for if she had traffic on the canal would have been considerably impeded, if not entirely stopped until she had been raised. The Hannah Beckett belongs to the Anglesea Limestone Co., and contained a cargo of macadam from Petmanmcewr, for Runcorn Docks, Mr. Thomas being the broker. Captain Metcalf, of the Telegraphic, which belongs to Messrs. G. L. Tap- Scott and Co., of London, states that his vessel has escaped without any damage whatever.

NEW BOOKS, MAGAZINES, ETC.

MACMILLAN AND CO.—These publishers deter- mined wisely when they adjudged "Tom Cringle's Log" (Michael Scott) a place in their series of Illustrated Standard Novels. There are no doubt, many yet, so unfortunate as to be totally unac- quainted with this pseudo-autobiography. Like Marryat and Cooper, Michael Scott's ability runs largely seaward, though in the " Log " is to be found rdany a true and striking description of incident on land. His pictures of sea and seafaring life are beyond question, extremely vivid and picturesque, while in many instances the scene changes from impending tragedy, dark and terrible, to that of frolicsome and boisterous mirth. To the present edition, which in common with the others of the same series is handsomely bound, is a capable intro- duction by Mowbray Morris. This latter deals with the author's parentage and life, and includes a clever description of Scott's inimitable penman- ship. There are numerous illustrations, in which are embodied the notable characters in the story with faithful observance of detail and peculiarities. For these excellent rspresentations the reader is in- debted to Mr. J. Ayton Symington. HORACE Cox.—" The London Home Monthly " for April is replete in articles of varied description, but one type of excellence—the first. At the commencement are reproduced from the Lyceum Souvenir of "King Arthur," Mr. Bernard Patridge's fine representations of Mr. Henry Irving and Miss Ellen Terry as " King Arthur" and " Guinevere." These two drawings form the first instalment of a programme of contents which it would be hard to excel. Among the more particular are the follow- ing : " The Law as a profession," by four gentlemen quite capable of explaining the subject to the full; Police of the World," by George Ives; "The Boy- hood of Tennyson," by Arthur Waugh ; and lastly, a topic of prevailing interest—" Hypnotism and its Future," which is the outcome of a talk with an expert. The next number will contain the opening 'chapters of a short serial, entitled "Unto the third and fourth generation," by Hall Caine. JAMES Wmusro.—The 22nd edition of Willing's " British and Irish Press Guide " and advertisers' directory and handbook is incorporated with the present year. The uses of this book to advertisers, publishers, pressmen, and persons of almost every grade of trade, are innumerable, and all the infor- mation contained is thoroughly reliable, and com- prises the chief particulars of the press of the United Kingdom. Indeed we may conclude that what its compilers don't know is not worth know- ing. The price, Is., does not seem at all compatible with the amount of information obtainable, but we're not going to grumble. F. V. WHITE AND CO.—lnstesd of having to wait till Christmas we have this month received " The Major's Favourite " by John Strange Winter, who as usual clothes her, hero in a red Coat. Taken on the whole there is not quite as much variety and detail in the book, as in some of its predecessors. In it the author has endeavoured, and succeeded, in showing "the struggles of a young mind reared with all care and love, struggles between honour and affection, between right and wrong, between a conception of stern duty and the strength of faithful friendship." The whole foundation of the book turns upon the dead body of a huge St Betnard dog named Maxsie. Though of seeming triviality, the story retains its I interest, as the problem "who killed the dog," deepens. Carmine Drummond, the heroine, unfor- tunately becomes possessed of what she considers to be the solution to the question and which con- cerns the honour of the person whom the girl holds to be her dearest friend. This root of distrust causes an undefined estrangement, which an unlooked for admission on the part of another person in the story serves to heel up. T. FISHER UNWIN.—In the' Century "for April the item to which particular attention is drawn is a character sketch of Madame fiejane by Justin Huntly M'Carthy. The sketch though short, is com- prehensive and contains the following description of the actress's many qualities ;—" Madame RZijane is an actress endowed with many qualities: She has charm, vivacity, grace—even the grace to be ungracious; she can dominate a very marked per- sonality, and keep it in subservience to the part which she creates; she has playfulness, pathos,ten- derness, humor,—she has even a degree of passion :compatible with the ascendancy of the playful, the pathetic, the tender, and the humorous qualities. Just as much of the tragic tone as it is permitted to the Comic Muse to mingle with her mood of laughter and her mood of tears—lust so much Madame Rejane may claim to include among her gifts." The remaining contents of the number do but further tend to strengthen the character this magazine has gained for excellency of literature, letterpress, and illustration. GEORGE NEWNES IND CO.—This month's "Strand" is a number par excellence. First of all there com- mences in it that long-announced series of stories by A. Conan Doyle.entitled " The Exploits of Briga- dier Gerard." The first and the one in the present number is "How the Brigadier held the King," and is a very lively pourtrayal of Gerard's adventures, with diabolical Spanish brigands, and less savage but as formidable British dragoons. In the story there is much dry humour, while the hero's conceit is about on a par With his impudence. If No. 1 is but the forerunner of others of equal interest, it is no exaggeration to predict that ere long they will have a name equal to the same author's "Sherlock Holmes " series. lhis month's story from the diary of a doctor is also specially good, while the remaining quota of items is equally a credit to the publishers. FREDERICK WARNE AND O.—A handsomely- bound(2, 6114 volume has been issued by these pub- , lishers, containing three stories by the Rev. Silas K. Hocking. 1 hey are entitled " Doctor Dick," " John Silent," and " Will Vivian's Story," though the same characters run through each. " Doctor Dick "is the one by which the book is designated, and is a story raised above the ordinary, mainly by its elevation of tone and breadth of treatment. Mr. Hocking is styled as one of the not too numerous band of novel-writers who know how to convey re- ligious teaching without. wearying the reader. Its "Doctor Dick" the author chooses as his hero a man drhose main failing is the far too common one of intemperance. Throughout the story his refor- mation steadily progresses, urged on by the benefl- zial influence of the heroine, Irene Bevill. As usual, Mr. Hocking pitches the scene of his narrative in Cornwall, where the nieural hu- mour and superstition of the people pro- vides a never-Failing theme for the novelist. Speaking of the superstition, a very amusing refer- ence to Lancashire women is made by a " crank " in the novel who, having heard that the heroine is a Lancashire girl, comes out with the—to us—con- vulsing statement "the' do say that a lot or the Lankishire sv, men be witches," Many other asser- tions and suppositions are made which proclaim the author to he a man with a deep store of knowledge of the composite nature., of the inhabitants of these widely divided counties. : here are several good illustrations and the let re? prima quite does justice to the excenence of t,be story.
