The War Tire war between China and Japan in the Eist. is now, it appears, at an end. The terms of peace have been decided upon at conferences between representatives of the two powers, and a treaty signed. The penalty which China has to pay for her weakness and unpreparedness is a severe one. She cedes to Japan the large island of Formosa which lies off the Chinese coast, besides several smaller islands ; and in addition to this Japan is to retain posses- sion of a large slice of the mainland which her soldiers now occupy. China has also to pay a war indemnity of about 30,000,000, and to throw open seven new ports to foreign commerce. This treaty will, it may be expected, settle the differ: ences between China and Japan for seine years to come at all events, but, unfortunately, it raises lother questions which may be much more serious for the Western world than the conflict between the two Eastern nations. The question that is now being asked is, will Russia agree to the rati- fication of this treaty ? It has all along been known that there were at least three European countries whose interest might be affected by the war in the East, especially as soon as the demands of the Japanese come to be discussed. These three countries are England, Russia, and France,but the interests of England and Russia far transcend those of France, and this latter country might probably be left out of account but for the fact that they may think it policy to back up the claims of Russia. Fortunately, judging from the terms of peace as yet made public, they do not in any way call for interference or remonstrance on the part of England. In fact, the throwing open to commerce of additional Chinese ports must in every respect be an advantage to the country which controls more than eighty per cent. of the foreign trade of the Chinese Empire. The session of Formosa will, no doubt, tend to strengthen the naval power of a go-ahead people such as the Japanese are proving themselves to be, but the English Government is not likely to display any selfish jealousy in regard to that. With Russia the case is different. It is almost certain that the Northern Colossus will strongly object to at least one clause in the proposed treaty. The installing of a comparatively strong military and naval power like Japan upon the Chinese mainland will have thp effect of delaying if not of permanently thwarting the carrying out of one of Russia's schemes for an extension of territory which has long been openly projected. At the present time tke great naval station of Russia in the Far East is Vladivostok, but this port, which is the oily $O. outlet for millions of square mites of territory, is frozen solid from November to March, a fatal drawback to. its complete usefulness either in peace or war. The aim of the Russians has, there, fore, all along been to obtain from China an in- crease of territory to the south and with that territory a harbour which would answer all their purposes much better than the ice-girt Vladivos- tok. By the conclusion of the present treaty between China and Japan serious obstacles are thrown in the way of the carrying out of this Russian ambition. Russia will not now merely have to reckon with China but also with the much more formidable power of Japan. It may be, therefore, that she will at once intervene and at- tempt to prevent the Japanese from stepping in between her and her expected prize. It is due to this circumstance that the situation in the East is still regarded by some uneasirrass by European, and particularly by English statesmen. If Russia acquiesces it is in the highest degree improbable that any other power will interfere, and peace will soon be declared in the Far East. But should Russia veto the proposed terms of settlement the Japanese people will have cruel alternatives to choose from—either to forego what they probably regard as the most important spoils of victory, or to face another and a much more formidable war.

THE split in the Unionist Party is A Political Disruption, developing with remarkable

rapidity. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN has lately been treated with indecent gratitude by a large section of the Tories. They have described him as a " demagogue " and as a " political gamb- ler "with little or no principle, and he has been ' girded at almost daily in some of their newspapers. This is Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S reward. He has fought against Home Rule with more .vigour and more ability, and, lot it be said, with more bitterness and acrimony, than all the Tory leaders put to- gether, and the result is that Tories are now rounding upon him and declaring that he is nothing better than a" polit.cal gambler." While Lord SALISBURY has been enjoying a "lettered leisure," at Hatfield, and while Mr. BALFOUR has been frivolling his time away at golfing links or in learn- ing to ride a bicycle in some oft-street , in an aristo- cratic quarter of London, Mr. CHAMBERLAIN has, in season and out of season, been denouncing Irish len and their claims, yet his only reward is that of being dubbed a demagogue.. But, after all, Mr. CHAMBERLAI" is not the man to stanrl

these buffetings without an attempt to retur.ir them in kind, and the consequence that his orczan th e, "Birmingham post' has some remarkable plain things to say about the " Tapers and Tadpoles ".of the Tory party. "Everybody," it says, " knows who are the critics and the carper's, at what value they may be justly rated, to what class they belong. They have always infested and sometimes embarrassed the Tory party. Mr. DISRAELI drew them with fine contemptuous strokes in his novel of Coningsby.' He gave them generic names Taper and Tadpole—which have ever since kePt place in political literature, and are as well merited and as readily applied to-day as they were fifty years ago." "These people belong, we are further told, "to the small official class and to their hangers-on—those who receive the offices in a Government, and whose devotion to, party, if not founded upon solicitude for personal interests, is quickened by that not too exalted sentiment" But, the Birmingham paper furthe,r, points out,that while these " parasites of politics may be of no account themselves, if their actions are not checked and discountenanced by the Tory leaders the result may be serious—nothing less serious in fact than the retirement from Parlia. Mellt of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN himself. con- -4‘ It is smaller ceivable," we are told, "that a stateman who desires power only that it may be used for the double purpose of preventing national disaster snd of promoting remedial social :egislation, if be were thwarted by those for whose alliance he has insT° enormous personal sacrifices, might prefer to retire from a barren parliamentary career, embitteredb); i insult and undermined by intrigue, and /ngli f prefer to commit the fortunes of the great causebee reform to the direct support of the masses of t a people." When language like this is used il the newspaper which, it is well-known, speaks the mind of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN" himself it is quite eie; that the Unionist alliance has been shaken te 1 foundation.

RUNCORN PARISH CHURCH SUNDAY SCHOOLS. An entertainment, the proceeds of which were in aid of the prize fund in connection with the above-men- tioned Sunday Schools,was held in the National Infants' schoolroom, on Thursday evening. There was a good attendance. The programme was provided entirely by Mr. Mattie Cheshire and "Little Aubrey " Cheshire,both of whom efficiently acquitted themselves in a kumorous, musical, and dramatic recital. That the audience thmoughly appreciated the efforts of the entertainers was attested by the frequent applause during the two hours' unique performance. THE NATIONAL UNION OF TEACHERS' CONFERENCE. The 26th annual conference of the National Union of Teachers has been held during the past week at the Central Hall, Manchester. The representatives of the Runcorn and District Association were Messrs. J. F. Moulton (Runcorn), R. Cookson (Weston Point), W. Epps (Moulton near Northwich), T. Skowan, (representing St. Helens Association). The sum raised by the Runcorn and District Association in aid of the charities of the Unien was £l7 us., being an increase of £4 on the previous year. This sum was made up as follows : Greenway-road Board School, £8 ; Runcorn Wesleyan 155.; Newtown Infants, 153.; Weston Point, lie.; Weston Wesleyan, 69.; Halton Grammar School. lie.; Mill Brow Board School, ss. ; Moulton Board School, £1 Us. 6d. ; Widnes West Bank Board School, £1 179. 01. DEATH OF MISS UPTON, RUNCORN.

Miss Upton, 19 years of age, daughter of Mr. William York-st., died after a brief illness on Uptch...'.. Monday The ~eceased was associated with the Primi- tive Methodist o".Pel, Greenway-road. She was a 1.. member of the choir ,and was also connected with the i Sunday school. She tied .gained the respect and esteem of a large circle of friends, I,nd much sympathy is ex- tended to the bereaved family. The funeral of the de- ceased took place on Thursdey afternoon. A large assemblage of people met at the doceased'a late residence arid afterwards walked in proeession to the Primitive Methodist Chapel, Greenway-road, vrhere a short service was held. Prior to their departuee the hymn " Safe in the Arms of Jesus " was sung. The procession was headed by the Rev, T. Bramall, Rev. H. R. Didcock,Mr, Sayle, and Mr. Hillidge (superintendents), followed by Mr. fleet (secretary), Mr. Aston (treasurer), and the following members of the congregation e—Messrs. Hill, Bradshaw, Hayes, Jabrz Stubb., Peter Efillidge, W. Miles, Pilgrim, Davies, Sayle, jun., A. and F. Shingler, Nickson, Jackson, Beswick, Dobson,Efoxworth,Howard, Davies, Richardson, and Garner. There were also a number of the members of the Christian Endeavour Clasp, and several connected with the Sunday school. Mr. W. M. Morris and Mr. J. J. Shaw were present from the Co-operative Society,and Mr.R. Morris,the deceased's former employer, also walked. The relatives present were :—Mr. Upton (tather),Fred, Sydney, Mark, Harold, and Gertrude (brothers and sister), Mrs. Steeier and Miss Stonier, Mrs. Barrow, Mrs. Mair and Mr. G. Hawley. The coffin was borne by the following : Messrs.- Dyer, Cooke, Robert Stubbs, Dutton, W. Gittens jun.,. H. Woodward, Johnson, and Kilcross. The service in • the church was commenced by singing the hymn" Thou' , knowest Lord the weariness and sorrows." The Bev.. , H. R. IMcock engaged in prayer and Mr. Bramall react the funeral service. The hymn "Shall we meet beyond , the river" was sung. Mr. W. Gittens played the " Dead ; March "in Saul as the people left the chapel. The ser- ' vice at the grave side was conducted by the Rev. T. Bramall. A number of beautiful wreathe had been sent by the following :—Father and Mother, sister and ' brothers, Mr. and Mrs. W. Rushton, Mr. and Mrs. Brad- shaw and family, Runcorn Co-operative employes, M.A. Leyland, Mr. and Mrs. Littlemore, Miss M. A. Little- more, Mr. and Mrs. C. F. Kniveton, employe; of Mr. R. Morris, Mr. and Mrs. Hoxwerth, Mr. and Mrs. Miles. A couple of artificial wreaths had also been subscribed for by members of the Christian Endeavour class, and the Sunday school.

RUNCORN BURIAL BOARD. RATEPAYERS' MEErING LAST NIGHT.

On Thursday evening the adjourned meeting of rate- payers for the purpose of receiving the Burial Board bal- ance sheet and auditors' report was held in the Bethesda Lecture Room. Mr. William Handley occupied the chair, and th,re were also present :—Messrs. W. Mellor, J. B. Digger, J. Pritchard, W. Jones, T. Cook, and W. Sutton (ass ista nt overseer). The Chairman said their business wassimply to receive the auditors' report of the Burial Board accounts. They had seat in their certifieate dated April ith, as follows " We have this day completed the audit of the books of :the Runcorn Burial Board, and have had vouchers pro- dused for all payments, and find the same correct in every particular." The certificate was signed by Joseph K.- Digges and John Poole. The balance sheet showed that the year commenced with a balance on general account of £3 2s. 7d. ; and on sink- ing fund account £215 13s. id. The year concluded with the sum of £BO 15a. sd. due to bank en general account. The disbursements were :—By inter- est on mortgages £lBl 6s. Bd., mortgages paid off £3OO, maintenance and repairs £lB 17s. 4d., rates, taxes, and tithes £l2 13s. 4d., printing and stationery £1 14s. 6d., sundries and expenses £2O 03. 9d., wages £202 4s. 10d., bank commission 16s. 2d., balance in bank on Sinking Fund Act £45 17s. Bd. ; total, £783 11s. 3d. The ba- lance on the sinking fund account was £312 188. 4d., and mortgage account £4,884 3s. Be said the mort- gages would be paid off about the year 1910, so that un- til that time there would very likely be a small call upon the rates of something less than ld. in the L. That WAS a very small charge for a cemetery that had served them so long, and with so much land for future extension which would stand the necessities for Runcorn for many years to come. Mr. Mellor proposed the adoption of the accounts. Mr. Pritchard seconded, and it was carried unanimously. The Chairman said on go.iking up the minutes he found that the first meeting was held on the 4th June, 1857. It was a singular fact—and yet cot singular— that of those who attended the first meeting there was only one gentleman that was now living. That was their good old friend Mr. John Simpson, who was a member of the first Burial Board. The overseers at the time were William Rigby and Peter Ormrod,and Mr. Edward Shaw was the assistant overseer. The Board was to consist of nine members and an old custom that ob- tained in Runeorn was observed. Six members were proposed and seconded and carried by the meeting un- animously as members of the Burial Board. His (the chairman's) father was among the six. About six other persons were proposed for the remaining three seats. A poll was demanded, and a very exciting one it was. It was held on the 20th June 1857 and the result was t Mr. Thomas Johnson, 559 ; Mr. John Linaker Wright, 354 ; Mr. Josiah Rigby, 553. At the time the Cemetery site was chosen there were only three cottages and the Vine Hotel beyond Savage's Bridge- There was no way to the Cemetery and the reason it was now called Green- way-road was because there was a path a little to the left of the present road called the" Greenway." The whole of the town above Savage's Bridgahad grown within the last 37 years, and this extension of the town bad come about from making the cemetery. The Chairman pointed out that this was the last vestry meeting that would be held for this purpose. In the future the accounts would come under the supervision of a public auditor. When they considered that the cemetery cost £lO,OOO, and £5,000 was now owing, and that all work- ing expenses had been paid and many improvements carried out of revenue, without unduly burdening the rates, they must conclude that the business had been well conducted and that they had great cause for con- gratulation. The cemetery did not cost any cottager sixpence a year in rates, and as a large number of people used it as a park it was worth mere than sixpence a year to them for the comfort and pleasure they derived from perambulating the walks and seeing the flowers. Mr. John Poole said it wash remarkable fact that Mr. Salkeld was the first secretary and treasurer of the Board in 1658 and still held the position. Mr. W. Mellor said he had been a member of the Burial Board for many years, and had found Mr. Salkeld a most genial gentleman. In his opinion the business of the Burial Board had been conducted in a very economi- cal manner, and to the general satisfaction of the rate- payers If the District Council continued to manage it in the same manner the ratepayers would have no cause for complaint. Mr. Pritchard moved that the cordial vote of thanks of the vestry be tendered to Mr. Salkeld for his long and faithful services in connection with the Burial Board.— ' Mr. Mellor seconded the motion.—Mr. Digges supported the motion, and said the books had been kept in a mag- nificent mannor.—The motion was carried unanimously. It was mentioned that Mr. Sutton had been assistant overseer for 16 years' and on the proposition of Mr. Digges, seconded by Mr. Poole, he was heartily thanked for his services as vestry clerk. —Mr. Sutton replied, and the meeting concluded with a vote of thanks to the Chairman.

THE EXAMINER. SATUR I )11 Y, APR IL 20 1895 THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

SCALDING FATALITY AT RUNCORN.

SHOCKING DEATH OF A WORKS' MANAGER. Mons. J. R. Phulpin, a French gentleman, who was manager of the Australian Alum Works, Runcorn, met with serious injuries at the works on Thursday after- noon, and which culminated in his death at his resi- dence, Rock Cottage, Frodsham, the following morning. The deceased was engaged with a workman in fixing a syphon to drainoff the contents of a large vat of liquid ; when he over-balanced himself and fell into the vat. The liquid at the time was at a temperature of 185 degrees. He was so severely scalded that little hope was held out for his recovery. He suffered great agony up to the time of his death. Mr. H. Churton, coroner, held an inquest at the Ring o' Bells, Overton, on Saturday afternoon, on the body of the deceased. Mr. A. Linaker appeared on behalf of the deceased's wife, and Mr. McCullock was also present from the works.—Edward Owen, liquor runner, at the Australian Alum Works, Runcorn, deposed that on Thursday morning, about 11.45, he and deceased were in the at of syphoning a vat to run out the liqUer, which atood at a temperature of 185 der166,4. It was over 41t. deep. Deceased wishing to bend a pipe got a stick whilst witness was going down &me steps for a bar for the purese. Mr. Owen had only just turned his back When he heard a splash, and found that the manager had fallen WO the Vat threugh the piece Of woo 0 l'eak- ing. The instrument uadd Was Scarcely fit to bear a kettle. Deceased was a man of great experience, but in doing this part of the work he was not perform- ing his proper duties. The liquor was sulph- ate of alumina cr free acid. He was very severely scalded, although rescued within a few moments. He was speedily under the attendance of Dr. McDougall, who, having attended to him, wrapped him in flannels. Be was subsequently taken home to Frod- sham, where he died at three o'clock on Friday morn- ing. No one but himself was responsible for his death. He planned the scheme adopted. He was 31 pears of age, dying on his birthday. Witness could not say that 1 a similar action had been previously done. He would certainly have not used such a stick for the purpose.— This was the only evidence called, and the Coroner re- ferring to the action of the deceased, said it was a very improper thing for him to do, for the evidence showed that the stick was unequal to raise a heavy weight. The natural result was that the stick broke, and he was pre- cipitated into the vat of alum liquid, and it being of a temperature of 185 degrees he was fearfully scalded, so that it would have been impossible for him to survive. they could not blame anyone, because no one was res- ponsible but the deceased, who did a most highly im- proper and imprudent thing. Their only verdict could be one of accidental death.—Mr. Linaker said he was prepared to adduce evidence to the effect that Mons. Phulpin told his wife that it was his own fault.—A. ver- dict of "Accidental death ,r was accordingly returned. The remains of the deceased were conveyed from the residence at Frodsham to Runcorn on Wednesday morn- ing, when a short burial service was conducted in the St. Edward's Roman Catholic Church prior to their de- parture to London en route for France. The cortege was met at Halton Brook by the workmen employed at the alam works, of which the decased was manager. They 'walked in procession to the church. The service was conducted by the Rev. Dr.. Gastaldi and Fattier Hoary, of llantwich. The chief mourners present were : Mons. Jean Joseph Louis Phulpita (brother), Mons. Hottelet Louis joseph Deiire (father-in-law), Mr. and Mrs. H. G. Linaker, Miss Matteson, Mr. Richardson, Miss and Miss A. Richardson. A start was afterwards made for the railway station, where the cortege was mett by Mrs. Phulpin(the deceased's wife),Madame Desire,and others. The coffirr was covered with a number of floral tributes which had been forwarded by the workmen at the alum works, Mr. and Mrs. H. G. Linaker, Rev. and Mrs. Blogg, Mr. ad Mrs. Smith, Mr. and Mrs. Rhodes, Miss Andrews, and Miss Mallesorr. The coffin was despatched by the 12:25 train to London. The workmen present, some Of whom acted as bearers, were : Messrs. W. .McCullock,,J; !Dutton, W. Taylor, D. Edwards, E. Jones, J Shaw, T. Bulpin. W. Barker, J. Gaze, T. Booth, T. Hatton, I. Bennett,•A. Hampson, W. Snape, H. Helsby, T. Jones, W. Thomas, H. Barker, E.. Owens, T. Barker, W. Worrel, H. Woman, J. Corker, J. Jephson, J. Jack- son and F. Johnston.

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

The rumour which was so prevalent at Runcorn last week to the effect that what are commonly known as Wiggs' Chemical Works,were to be closed, has not yet been carried into effect, and there is reason to believe that the directors of the United Alkali Company have thought it advisable to modify their decision, for there is no doubt that they had decided to shut the works up. Taking into consi- deration the present feeling in the town for com- mencing new chemical works in the event of the present ones being closed,it is perhaps a wise policy on the part of the United Alkali Company to keep their Runcorn works going, even if only on short time. It will doubtless prevent the determination for the provision of new chemical works becoming an accomplished fact. There is a strong opinion that the chemical trade if properly conducted at Runcorn can be made a profitable investment, and if the United Alkali Company think otherwise and close their works, an attempt to make it ao, will iu all probability be declded upon,

The Frodsham vestry meeting on Monday was of a rather lively character. Mr. Robert Foulkes has for some time now been to the fore at these annual meetings with his protests against what he terms the Romanising of the Church of England. His " low " church views are diametrically opposed to the " high " churchism of the Vicar of Frodsham. He took objection to the surplices used by the choir; the decorating of the church with flowers ; the chanting of the responses to the ten command- ments; the introduction of hymns "Ancient and Modern,' some of which he contended were popish, teaching baptismal regeneration, the worship of Mary, apostolic succesion, etc. These are some of his many objections. Although but few would be prepared to coincide with him in all the points he raised and protested against, yet it is apparent that the Established Church shows signs of pitching its tents a day's march nearer Rome. At one time a great deal was heard of the Church of England being the" bulwarks of Protestantism," but it is to be feared that if it ever were so, it would not now be very secure.

A notable feature of the Runcorn Parish Church

vestry meeting was the very long metre the Vicar gave his audience in his address. He surely must have satisfied the most zealous churchman present. A good number of points were touched upon, and the address was tinged with a little quiet sarcasm. It transpires that in one of the charities the sum of is. 7d. is given for distribution amongst the poor. In referring to this he said as it was not an eccle- siastical charity he took it for granted that Mr. llinison and thy Council would able to have a voice in the distribution of the Is 7d., and no doubt that would be highly gratifying to them. If they would appoint a trustee to look after the Is. 7d. he would be most glad to appoint another so that the money would not go astray. Of cAirse, it is all very well for the Vicar to poke fun at sash a body as the Council doling out the small sum of Is. 7d., but as the Vicar admitted, in the course of a few years , another ctrarity must come under the Council's care, and in course of time there may be a goodly sum to distribute, which, perhap& will be done as well, if not better, than has been the case by repre- sentatives of the Church of England.

The Vicar had also•something. to say about church rates. "If a church rate were laid in Runcorn," he said, "it would be like calling upon the spirits from the vasty deep." From this it may be presumed that he is speaking from experience. Ht. has found that the levying of church ratesuiow that they have become voluntary, is a complettrfailure, and that in the event of the Parish church requiring repair or 'improvement, the funds would have to, come through a very different channethan by &church rate.. From this point the Vicar proceedeclto tell his hearers that the Established Church wasrwhat he might call Socialism applied to• Christianity. "The Established Church was socialistic in-so-much as it provided religion for everybody who liked to make use of it free." The same argument might be used' with regard to the other churches, for what- ever is given towards their support, is done so vol- untarily. A socialist would place himself in a very uncomfortable position, even in the Established Church, if he resolutely refused to contribute, when. the collection plate came round with its usual regu- larity.

SEAMEN'S INSTITUTE, RU NCO RN..

The annual report for this Institute has just been sued. It has bcen prepared by Mr. William Shaw, rnis-1 sionary, and the following extracts may be of general, interest :—" I am thankful to say that the work at this - Branch has not been without encouragement during the year 1894, for, although for the most part the work,here• as elsewhere, is literally bred cast upon the watersond many days may pass before the fruit appears, yet as the years go by the Master is pleased to allow some results to be seen. My visits to the vessels and canal boats have been welcomed Isy 01, and my humble services, in many cases of accident and death among the floating popula- tion during the year, have been thankfully received. I have assisted several to the hospital, and, in case of death,l have been enabled to assist in having the bodies removed to their homes. In two cases,where they have been interred in Runcorn, they have been buried in the 'Sailors' Grave.'"

"The Institute work has this year been very gratifying from the way in which the men have attended the ser- vices in the evenings during the cold winter months. The Institute is a great boon to the men." " The meetings at the Mission Church are very well attended, and there is, no doubt sailors like a good hearty service in which they can "The Sunday School lam thankful is well attended and! believe God is blessing this branch of the work. The Band of Hope meetings have been well attended, and I am pleased to say that Mr. Albert Abel is now taking a great interest in this particular work." " The weekly entertainments and magic lantern lec- tures have been this year successful. Seamen and canal men communicants, 12; teachers and workers, 147 ; pledges taken, 22 ; bags of reading given, 136; letters wriden, 302. EASTERTIDE AT RUNCORN.

Beautifully fine weather prevailed during Eastertide,. and the holidays have been spent in a joyous manner. Special services were held on Friday at the various churches. At the evening service at the Parish Church • a service of song entitled " The Man of Sorrows "w rendered by the choir, under the conductorship of Mr. James Littler. At St. John's Presbyterian Church,a tea and musical entertainment was held in the schoo'room on Good Friday. A good number partook of tea under the presidency of the Rev. J. Pulford Williams-(pastor). An interesting programme was gone through. Songs were given by Mrs. Temperley, Miss S. P. Hayes, and Dr. HarrOp :violin solo, Miss Temperley ; piano duet, I Miss Pope and Miss Harrop ; mandoline duet, the Ittisses r Warburton ; piano solo, Miss M. Jones ; and a duet by Mr. and Mrs. Burns. Glees were given by a party con- sisting of Mrs. T3mperlev, Mrs. R. Yates, and Miss. S. P. Hayes, Mr.R.Yates, Mr.T. Charleston, and Mr.A. Robin- son. On Sunday, the churches were tastefully decorated, Lenten lilies being the principal floral adornment intro- duced. At the Parish Church the musical part was more than usually interesting, the air " But Thou did'st not ' leave his soul in hell" (Mr. James Littler singing the solo) and the chorus " Worthy is the Lanala that was slain " were given with great effect. Following the sermon came the " Hallelujah Chorus." At the Wesleyan Churches the great festival was also recognized and appropriate sermons preached. At St.PauPs,Halton-road and Camden Wesleyan Chapels special pieces were sung by the choirs. At the Congregational Church addresses appropriate to the day were given by the Rev. J. Simp- son. The choir rendered anthems, Misses N. Lunt and Rodgers gave a duet, and a quartette was given by Miss Maggie Hetherington, Messrs. Gowanlock, E Worrall, and Lunt. St. Ed ~vards Catholic Church was also neatly decorated, and the services were well attended. At the Holy Trinity Church special services were held —On Monday large numbers of people left the town for various places. The chief attraction at Runcorn was the football match between Widnes and Runcorn, and the game was witnessed by a large concourse of people.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The annual meeting of this Board was held on Tues- day at Dutton Workhouse. The members of the Run- corn Rural District Council were present in addition to the Rev. Canon Wood, Messrs. S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., A. R. Norman, S. Yarwood, Mr. J. Ashton (clerk), and M. G. Ashton (deputy clerk).—Mr. Heywood proposed that Mr. Knowles be the chairman of the Board. Mr. Jones seconded the motion.—Mr. S. Taylor supported, and the motion was carried unanimously.—Mr. Knowles acknowledged the honour conferred upon him.—Three persons were proposed for the position of vice-chairman. viz.: Mr. Heywood, Mr. Stokes and Mr. Brandreth. The two former gentlemen retired in favour of Mr. Brandreth who was unanimously elected. Tho Local Government Board in reply to an inquiry as ' to whether having regard to the time fixed by the Local Crovernruent Act, 1894, for the appointment of Overseers by parish nooncils, the Guardians might make their con- tribution orders on the present Overseers, stated that the contribution orders of the Guardians must be ad- dressed to the Overseers by name who were in office when the orders were issued and as it seemed doubtful whether the Guardians could enforce any orders against the new Overseers which were not issued to them, the Board thought that the Guardians sheuld,if practicable, defer the 118n0 of contribUtion emlers till the new Over- ii Were appointed. It was reported that there had been two applications in response to an advertisement for an assistant matron. One had failed to attend the meeting,and the other after visiting the house had declined the situation.—Mr. Priestner said he was very glad as that would end the matter.—Mr. Norman said the Ladies' Committee were very persistent in their recommendation that the matron required assistance. She had the whole of the cutting out of the Clothing for the children. He was in favour of economy, but protested against their being too bird upon the officers and the house. A long discussion after- wards took place,taken part in by Mossrs.Roylance,Tay- lor, Stokes, and Brandreth. It was ultimately decided on the motion of Mr. Woodward, seconded by the Rev. Canon Wood, that the whole Board consider the question at the next meeting of the Board.—Mr. Heywood pre- sented the accounts of the Finance Committee and cheques were signed for out relief as follows :—Runcorn, £95; Frodsham, £32 ; Budworth, £2o.—The Chairman said that after the last Board meeting Mr. Dansey visited the house with Dr. Fuller,medical inspector to the Local Government Board, and reported that it was imperative that another nurse be appointed without delay.

PENTCETH.

EASTER HOLIDATS.—Tho holiday' this year have been kept up as enthusiastically as ever, Easter seeming to be the popular holiday time of the year. The works were closed from Thursday night till Tuesday morning. The usual re-union of old schohrs and parents and friends,. took place at the Friends School on Good Friday,. the present, and old scholars taking part in a game of foot- ball in which the old sesolars were beaten. • C. Bbrton the popular Warrington threequarter was in the ranks of the defeated ones, the association game, however, being a trifle strange to tha Rugby man. The usual enter- tainment was given in the Lecture room in the evening, the programme being arranged by the scholars and friends. The annual tea meeting and concert was held at the Wesleyan School and was a decided Success, At the concert ,the Secretary's report for the year was read, followed by the distribution of prizes for attendance. Selections were played by the Wesleyan brass band,who had paraded the village during the afternoon, under the conductorship of Mr. J. Bowyer. The boat.race on Saturday drew a large number of spectators to the river side. The number of pleasure seekers who left the village on Monday for•various destinations was very large, cyclists especially being in evidence. CONFIRMATION SERVICR.—A. confirmation service was held on Wednesday afternoon a--; St. Mary's Cnurch, Sankey. There were 25 young ladies and girls and 12 men presentlo be confirmed. The Rev. R. Royston con- ducted, the ceremony being also attended 'by Rev. E. S. Jackson, vicar, and Rev. J. R. Jones. The Bishop ad. dressed a very earnest and impressive address to the confirinees. There was a large gathering of people to witness the interesting ceremony.

DRAMATIC ENTERTAINMENT AT HAYDOCK LODGE.

In order to raise funds towards the liquidation of the Ashton Church debt, a dramatic performance was given at Haydock Lodge on Wednesday evening. The stage at the end of the long room was appropriately fitted up, the drop scene, which had been specially painted by Miss F.N.Street, for the production, being very cleverly executed. It was a view of Billings Hill, as seen from Golborne Park gates, Haydock Lodge nestling amongst the trees as an intermediary, while the foreground was a freshlrcut corn field,the sheaves standing out in bold relief, the whole representation being quite natural. There was a very fair audience, consisting of the elite of the neighbourhood. An orchestra under the conduc- torship of Mr. R. H. Pearson,supplied the musical inter- ludes,the programme including :—Overture, "Bohemian Girl ; valse, "Casino Tanze'' ; barn dance, " Go. Bang " ; valse, " Liebestreue " ; gavotte, "La Duchesse." The productions were an original play in one act by Sydney Grundy, "In Honour Bound," and a farce in one act by J. M. Morton, Esq., " Poor Pali- ' noddy." In the former the characters were :—" Sir George Ca.rlyon, Q.C., M.P., Mr. Buchanan Brown ; " Philip Graham," Mr. T. Ellis ; " Lady Carlyon," Mrs. Street ; "Rose Dalrymple," Miss Mould. ..Che acting all round was really good, althouqh the p t!'t of "Philip" was characterised by a little stiffness. Mr. Brown was quite at home in his part. and Mrs. Street made an ex- cellent repentant wife. Miss Mould threw just enough vivacity into her ac!drig to rent‘r it pleasingly accept- able. In the farce of " Poor piiricodds,' the characters were :—" Mr. Pdlicoddy," Mr. Buchanan Brown ; " Captain O'Scuttle," Mr. Street ; " Mrs. Pilficoddy," .Miss Mould ; "Mrs. O'Scuttle," Mrs. Street ; " Sarah Blunt," Mrs. Buchanan Brown. The representation was perfectly enjlyable throughout, and the various char- acters were repeatedly applaaded. The -performances were repeated on Thursday evening.

ELECTION OF CHAIRMAN.

After the CLERK had taken the chair, he said that he was in receipt of a letter from Mr. S. Davies who was unable to be present owing to an attack of lumbago. He hoped that the Board would re-elect Mr. W. Houghton chairman for another year. He himself would be will- ing to take his position as senior vice chairman.—Mr. HIBTIERT then moved that Mr. William Houghton be re- elected chairman for the ensuing year. He said that he did not as a rule think that a chairman should hold the position for over twelve months.—Mr. A. J. JOLLEY in seconding the nomination said that lie thought that ! when they got a good man, they should keep him.—Mr. CLARK supported the nomination, which on being put to the Board was carried unanimously.—Mr. HOUGHTON, on taking the chair, thanked the members for again placing him in that position. During .the coming year, some important work would have to be done, as the Board had committed themselves to the improvement of the Infirmary, but he was glad that there was a very competent Board to do it. .

ELECTION or vice CHAIRMAN, Mr. KNOWLES moved that Mr. S. Davies be again senior vice chairman for the ensuing year.—Mr.Dignum seconded, and the resolution was carried unanimously. --Afr.lFlmingsr:moved the re-election of Mr. Piling as jrunior vice chairman.—Mr. Dignum seconded, and the resolution was carried unanimously.

POWERS OF THE LADTHE -COMMITTEE%

The CRAIRMAN moved, and Mr. CLARKE seconded the usiral standing rules wit' the addition of the following : " That, subject to any alteration the Board may decide upon, the several committees, inclusive of Ur Ladies' Committee, appointed by the present Board be re-ap- pointed and continue in office until the annual Board meeting in 1898, that the members of the Ladies'Coin- mittee have the same authority as that of the Guardians to visit either the Workhouse or Ind ustral Schools at any time, that each committee appoint thehr own chairman and vice-chairman, and such sub-committees as they deem necessary, and that except as to committees the number of members upon which is limited by statute, it shall be competent for any committee, upon any occa- sion of the ,sresenee of a Guardian who has not been elected by the Board a member thereof,. to addr for the occasion, the name of the Guardian to their list, as though he or she had been elected by the Beard a member of the committre."—' Mr.KNowLas said that he bad an amendment to move to the above resolution. lir appointing the Ladies' Com- mittee, the Board did not appoint them to discharge the duties of Guardians. They were appointed to do certain duties, but he felt strongly that the Guardians were elected by the ratepayers to manage the workhouse and to act on their behalf as Pborlaw Guardians. BA thought that if they elected other persons practically to discharge their duties, they were shirkiag their duties. He moved as an amendment that the members of the Ladies' Com- mittee be at liberty to visit either the house or the schools on four days a week, at such hours between nine in the forenoon and five in the afternon as thy shall appoint. The Ladies' Committee shall fur- furnish the officials with the days and times at which they have arranged to,visit the house or schools.. He did not think they ought to have the same privileges, as the Guardians had.—Mr. E: L.Bearr seconded the amend- ment.—Mr.A.J.JoLLEv-hoped that the Guardians would carefully weigh the matter before recording their votes. He thought it was extremely unfair that the amend- ments should be proposed in that form, and that the Ladies' Committee should lae thus hampered in the dis- charge of their duties. Those duties were already suffi. ciently unpleasant, but they had been discharged in an admirable manner. TneLadies Committee had already rendered most substantiatservice to the Guardians. He therefore sincerely hoped' that the Guardians would by a large majority reject the amendment. They ought to have confidence in the Ladies' Committee but the amend- ment-seemed to imply they had not. Fir did not wish to cast any reflection upon any officer, but if they did theirduty they had nothing..totiread from surprise visits but if there were officers in the Union not doing their duty, the sooner they were dealt with the bettea. The Board had appointed the LadieeChmmittee three months ago to, inspect certain parts. of the institution, therefore they ought not to try to hamper them, but give them the fullest recognition to enable them to do their work with perfect liberty. Be hoped that the motion would be carried almost unanimously,—Mrs. CLAY agreed with Mr. Jbiley and hoped that the amendment would be defeated.—Mr. BILLINGE spee strongly in favov.r of. the motion, Ile hoped the Board would not cripple the action of the Ladies' Committee.. He wished also to ask the Clerk whether the Guardians were at liberty to bring a friend to the House when they wished.— TheCansix said that no Guardian was at liberty to in- troduce a visitor into either the House or the schools. Of course it would be a matter of courtesy to allow him to do so, but he had no legal right.—The amendment was then put to the vote, and the following members voted for it Rev. Father Moore, Messrs. A. Jolley, 13. L. Bent,.T. Mather, J. W. Knowles, H. ilibbert, L. War- burton, 7. Against : All the remaining members of the Board. The amendment was therefore lost by a largo maiority, and the motion was then put to the meeting and carried.

TRANSFER a CHESHIRE PhRISHES.

- The CLERK reported that the arrangements for the - transfer of the Cheshire parishes to the Runcorn Union had been signed. The rateable value of the Union at the time of the transfer was £357,106, and they lost by the transfer £17,339, thus reducing their rateable- value to £330,767. At the present time, however, the rateable value had risen to a347332,and if the proposed extension of the borough of Warrington were granted,, they would have a further increase of £5,600. If Winwick asylum were built, their assessment would be further in- creased.

===== 0002601_18950420_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
PRICE OF MAXIIRE.

Mr. TwomPsoet asked what was the price of the manure which was supplied for the garden at the Workhous.—Mr. WARD said that it was stable manure and cost 78.6 d. per ton.—Mr. Thompson thought the price was excessive, and some of the manure which was brought in WFIS very wet.—Mr. WARN said that allow- ance had been made for that.—Mr. Caws also ex- pressed an opinion that the price was very high, but Mr.Hibbert on the other hand thought it was reasonable. The mattos was allowed to drop after some further dis- cussion.

WARRIN&TON BOAT RACE. The interest in the Warrington and Bank Quay boat race has this year shown no abatement,and on Saturday afternoon when it took place,there was a tremendous crowd fining the river to watch the boats come in. The-afternonn was extremely fine, and this without doubt added greatly to the num- ber of spectators. Another additional attraction thia year is the fact that the winner becomes the bolder of a fine silver cup, which has be,..-n supplied by Messrs. E. and A. Eustanee, jewellers, Sankey- et.. The cup is held for one year only, but should any boat win it for three consecutive years, it be- coines the property of the owner. There were ten competitors who entered For the race, but the St. George, 21ft., captained by J Green, did not take part. The following were the names of the re- maining nine contestants, with the cap Pains and dimensions ;—Pink,W.Warburton,ls feet ; Florence, W. Meal, 20 feet; Maggie, R. Burthem, 22 feet ; Wildflower, H. Warburton, jun., 22 feet; Woodcock, .J. Peers, 22 feet ; Merlin, H. Turner, 22 feet ; Siota, J. Lee, 24 feet ; Silver Cloud, J. Jefferson, 27 feet; Gipsy, J. Davison, 30 feet. Mr. F. True.- cot's acted as number taker, and Mr. J. Whitlow adjudicated. The boots were handicapped at half a minute per foot. They started from Widnes a little after flood tide, and had a slight breeze be- hind them. A. splendid run was made, but the Pink, which had the first start, kept the lead the whole of the way, and won the race by seven minutes before the next boat. The race finished at Atherton's Quay, near to Messrs. Monks, Hall, and Co.'s Works. The following was the order and the time at which the boats finished the run :-1, Pink, 3.3 ; 2, Gipsy, 310 ; 3, Maggie, 3.12; 4, Wildflower. 3.16 : 5, Woodcock, 3.16 k; 6, Siesta, 3.17; 7, Silver Cloud, 317 k; 8, Merlin, 3.191; 9, Florence, 321 k. The winner was greeted with loud cheers by the large crowd which lined the banks. On Tuesday the silver cup was presented to the winner at the Ship Inn, Liverpool-road,the club's headquarters. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

THEFT OF WEA RI G APPAREL. At the Borough Police Court on Thursday morning,last week before Alderman Sutton and Lieut. Col. Ridgway, WalterHurnphries,3,Turner-st.,and John Wilson,3,Turn- er-st., were charged with stealing a shirt the property of Margaret Norman, Mersey-st., on Tuesday ; they were also charged with stealing a butcher's slop the property of Frederick John Carlisle,on Tuesday evening. Evidence for the first offence was given by Detective Monaghan who found the prisoners in a lodging-house known as the Black Horse. Witness brought them to the Police Station and charged them with stealing the shirt, value as., arid Humphries said" I took the shirt off the line. A few days after she missed a tablecloth and then mentioned the matter to prisoner who said " I have had apiece of blue paper sent me and I will get them out and make it right." UNREGISTERED MILEGNIALERS. —Joseph Thos. Marsden was charged with having on the 2nd April in Poulton carried on the trade of purveyor of milk without being registered under the Dairies and Cowsheds Order of 1885.—Mr. Peet. solicitor, Pleaded not guilty on behalf of defendant.--Mr. F. Kerfoot, nuisance inspector, said that the proceedings were taken under the above order. On the 2nd of April he saw defendant selling milk in Padgate-lane and ssked him if he was registered. De- fendant said he did not know of the regulation.—Mr. Peet's contention was that the proceedings should not have been taken under the order of 1885 as by that no penalty could be inflicted.—Mr. Kerfoot said that in 1886 the order was amended and a penalty could now he inflicted.—Defendant was fined ss. and costs.—William Ford was charged with a similar offence on the same date at the same place.—Mr. Kerfoot prosecuted,and de- fendant's point was that he had never received any notice about registration during the two years he had sold milk, otherwise he would have been quite willing to comply with the law.—A like penalty was inflicted in this case. Jenkins' solicitor, appeared at the Court and stated that Sarah Nevins held the license of the" Dog and Partridge" inn, in Manchester-road, for some years. She was a widow, but had again married, her husband being Thomss Francis Nevins, and on her marriage the license was vested in him. For some reason the husband had left his wife, and the whole property in the inn belonged to the wife as her separate estate. The owners of the inn, Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Company were willing that the tenancy of the premises shouli become re-vested in Mrs. Nevins." Mr. Jenkins asked them to allow Mrs. Nevins to have temporary authority to sell until the neat transfer day. There was ample security for any breach of the licensing acts as Mrs. Nevins had consider- able property in her own right, and the police were satisfied that any Prosecution would be amply met. The moment the temporary authority was grant ed,the license so far as the husband was concerned, W 39 lost. Mr. Nevins had signed on his own accord, the notices for the transfer and Mr. Jenkins had slrved the notices.—The application was granted. GRAVEL.

Gravel is a most painful disorder, caused by reten- tion in the kidneys of the salts which become crystal- lized into little sand-like formations, sooner -or later uniting into small gravel stones. The most severe pain accompanies an attack of gravel or kidney colic. The disease produces intense backache, painful lumbago; and a dragging sensation through the loins; MUCOUS, granular or ropy deposits in the urine with frequent desire to void. Warner's Safe Cure is a safe, sure, aad effectual remedy for gravel, because it cures the kidney disorder dissolves the stone, and removes every evidence of the disease DID NOT FORGET HIS NEIGHBOURS. Sellinge, or. Hythe, Stone Hill. By persevering with Warner's Sate Cure and Pills I passed five large gravel stones. My cure has caused me to recommend Warner's Safe Cure to my neighbours, who have also derived much benefit from its use. HENRY WOODLAND.

RUGBY. WARRINGTON v ST. HELENS RECREATION.

Warrington continuing their important holiday matches opposed St. Helens Recreation at Boundary- road St. Helens, on Saturday before 3,000 spectators. The homsters put a strong fifteen on the field whilst the Warringtonians were without Broady, Barber, Thornily, , and Hill. The Recs. kicked off and Evans returned ' sharply. The visitors held the advantage of the play for a short while forcing the leather to the Recs. territory. The Rees' forwards by fine passing succeeded in taking the ball to half way, where Boscow received and put his kick in, sending the ball back again to the home quarters. Warrington kept up the pressure and forced play to the Rees' territory but only for a short period for the ball was transferred to the Warrington line where a number of scrimmages and line outs took place. Seddon was eventually awarded a free kick in front of goal but Mclyneux failed to place the ball. A scrimmage took place just on the Warringtonians' goal line, The Recs. being almost successful till Warrington eventually touched down. The Rees' full back (Cre;sley) returned the kick and following up well nearly dropped a goal, the ball striking the goal posts. Warrington visited the Rees' quarters an advantage which was not long maintained. The leather was again forced away to half way where it settled for a few minutes. Fine bouts of passing between Trow and Traynor enabled the latter to take the leather to the Warrington twenty-five where he passed to Fildes, the latter scoring the first try. Molyneuv kicked a goal. Turner showed great strength in bringing the ball to the Rees' territory where the play remained for a considerable length of time, but the visitors were unable to score. The leather was again forced to half-way, where Boscow received and made his mark. Pilkington was loudly applauded for an excellent run to the Warrington 25 where he was knocked in touch by Burton. Passing between Bailey and Burrows enabled the visitors to take the ball back again to the Recs. quarters. Traynor again took possession and running a short distance passed to his comrades who failed to take advantage, thus allowing Burrows to come in, but the latter was pushed in touch before getting far. Open play ensued, the ball being returned from end to end. The half-time whistle was sounded with the score, St. Helens Recrea- tion 1-0-2, Warrington nil. Very little time was lost in changing position and the referee soon had them at woek again. Turner recommenced hostilities with the wind in his favour, and his kick off was sharply returned by the Rees' forwards who secured, thus causing great anxiety to Warrington, but .fortunately the ball was called back for a scrimmage. The ball was forced to Boscow'n hands who managed to get it well away. Saunders now came to the fore and passing to Taylor enabled the latter to get clear, but with no result. The ball was eventually kicked cle id. Notwithstanding the wind being in favour of Warrington the Recs. still preseed and forced play to mid- field. Dekin recsived and carried the leather to the Race.' territory, where hard lines were experienced. Scrimmages and line outs toek place just at the corner. Subsequently Burton secured and named to Houghton and Evans. Houghton again received and scored a well earned try. Burton kicked for goal,but his attempt was futile. The Rees.still preseed,and Boscow receiving made his mark. The Warring,tonians' goal was now in jeopardy, the visitors having to use all their strength to keep the scoring down. At length 'durton received but was sent intouch. The Recs. pressing more severely ferced the leather near to the visitors' goal line where Traynor scored A try. Molyneux again kickec' for goal hut was unsuccessful. Nothing daunted, the visitors forced play to the home quarters, but it returned to Boscow,who put the Warrington forwards in possession, hut they proved too weak, their opponents getting the hotter of them in an instant. The ball now travelled to mid-field, where the game was open and exciting, but only for a few minutes. Boscow again received from the hands of one of the Recs.' men and made his mark, but unfortunately failed to make a score. Burton got the leather after running the length of field was knocked into touch just when about to score, very hard lines indeed. A free kick was awarded to Warrington and J. W. Evans kicked but the Recs. charged it down. Warrington pressed as equally as the hotnesters, but wereonable to score. At length Burton had an opening, but was again stopped from doing any business. The final result came with the score, St. Helens Recreation, 1-1-5 ; Warrington, 0-1-1. The fol- lowing were the players who represented the teams : Warringten : Boscow, full back ; Burton, Bate, Cross aud Evans, three-quarter backs ; Foden and Houghton, half-hacks ; Turner, Taylor, Bailey, Swift, Saunders, °akin, Burrows and Holmes, forwards. WARRiNGTON A v. LANCASTER A.

The Warrington A team bad a pleasant time of it on Monday last, Bank Holiday, they having travelled to Lancaster where they opposed the A team of that town. The kick off was advertised for 10.30, but owing to tne late arriv of the Warringtonians the game did not com- mence until 11-30. WHY DOCTORS DIE.

Theoretically doctors ought to live for ever. In prac- tice—this in the double sense—they are short-lived. The popular supposition is that a medical man is so all wise that he can not only cure his own slightest ailmen, but that, by following the laws of hygiene, of which he is assumed to be absolute master, he can prolong his days beyond the normal and disproportionately with those of ordinary mortals. But the reverse is the case Familierity often breeds an indifference for his own methods, while the evil of even a trifling malady is augmented by his knowledge of its pathological possibilities, to which, of course, an ordinary sufferer would remain in blissful ignorance. But medical men likewise run other risks as a conse- quence of continuous exposure to disease. We learn, for instance, that among Russian medicos an abnormally high death-rate from tuberculosis prevails, fifteen per cent, of the whole number of deaths being assignable to that cause. Zelande has lately presented some moat valuable statistics calculated for three years, upon a con- s'deraticia of the whole profession (Russian), some fifteen thousand odd. Nearly thirty-three per cent, of deaths were due to virulent diseases—pyemia, typhus, cholera, diphtheria, &c. The prevalence of suicide—over eight per cent.—too, is strange.

===== 0002601_18950420_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW MUCH FOOD IS NECESSARY?

war in the East proves much with regard to the science of eating. The Japanese soldiers get rice, tinned and fresh meat, fish and vegetables. The Chinese troops are mainly restricted to a rice diet and what they can pilfer. Result: they cannot cope with their victorious opponents. The meat allowance of a Japanese soldier is seven ounces. The American standard is twenty ounces; the Russian, sixteen ounces; the English, twelve; the Italian, eleven; French, Belgian, Turkish and German, nine; Austrian and Spanish eight The. daily allowance of bread is highest among the Austrian troot:s, who receive thirty-two ounces, and lowest among the English troops, who get sixteen ounces. In the United States army, the French army, and the Italian army the allowance is twenty-two ounces. In the Ger- man army it is twenty-eight ; in the Russian seventeen. All modern soldiers, except the Russians, have a daily allowance of rice. The American army is the only one in the commissariat of which beans are an article of diet. The old theory that any man who has the strength te lift a gun and the skill to aim it directly at any enemy is good enough for a soldier, is exploded. We may mention here that bread and meat may form a perfect diet.

PAROCHIAL COMIIIITTRE.—The Appleton Parochial Committee's min Res were submitted as.: the meeting of the Rural District Council at Dutton Workhouse on Tues- day afternoon. The committee meeting was held on the 11th inst., and it was resolved to call the attention of the trustees of the Duke of Bridgewater to the Private Street Works Act, 1892, with respect to Cawdor-st., and the streets approaching Whitfield-road, Northwich-road, Bedford-st., Walton New-road, and that they be respec- tively requested to execute the works necessary to en- able the Rural District Council to declare such streets to be highways, repairable by the inhabitants at large. It was reconimenoed that £8 ss. be paid to the clerk, to remunerate him for his services from December 28th to March 25th. Mr. Farrington, nuisance inspector, sub- mitted his report.—The minutes were passed.

THE DISPUTE IN THE BOOT TRADE

At a special meeting of the National Federation of Boot and Shoe Manufacturers,held at Leicester on Wed- nesday last week full instructions were given to the Fei- eration representatives at the Board of Trade Confer- ence as to the line to be adopted on the resumption of proceedings. It was also decided to issue a notice to the operatives now at work that the Federation would loyally protect and support all who were now at workor who might resume work before a settlement. An appea has been made to English trade unions by the Nationa , Union of Operatives on behalf of the strike fund.

===== 0002601_18950420_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
ATHLETICS.

FOOTBALL.

The Easter matches which Warrington engaged in resulted in a win, a draw, and a loss, so that we were treated with absolute impartiality. The draw was a very creditable performance for Warrington for it was with the powerful Runcorn team who on Monday de- feated Oldham at Oldham. To draw with a team which Can do that is no mean performance. A very prominent feature with Warrington ha this season's fixtures has been the number of pointless draws they have made. This speaks volumes for their defence if it is not credit- able to their offensive powers. They have made point- less draws with St. Helens Recs., Broughton Rangers (twice),Wigan, Widnes, Kendal Hornets, and Runcorn, seven in all.

The match with Runcorn on Good Friday was a most pleasant and exciting one. Now that the two clubs have agreed to sink all differences and be friends again, it is to be hoped that they will continue to be so and that the fixture will be an annual one. To judge from Friday's match, there is every hope of this being so. I heard a Runcorn player say as he was leaving the field after the match that he was very pleased with the game, and that it was fought in a very fair and friendly manner. lithe spectators at Warrington and Runcorn will keep their tempers, there is not much danger of the 'players losing theirs.

With regard to the match itself,it may be said,although it is rather paradoxical, that each team should have won. They each in turn Pinned their opponents to the line, and how a big score on each side was not made is a mystery. Each team had the hardest of hard lines in about equal quantitv,so therefore the result is not unfair to either. Warrington were inferior in the forwards and half backs, especially the latter. Houghton and Foden did not come up to expectations in the least, and this is very strange as Houghton especially showed in recent matches some very good form. Perhaps it was an off day with him. When " Smack" Bate was put half back the Warrington team appeared to have acquired a new lease of life, and had the game continued much longer it would have gone hard with the Runcorn men.

Barber was unquestionably the best man of the whole thirty on the field, and was in exeeptionally fine form. His former club mate from Stockton Heath, Broady, made a most promising debut for Warrington, and by his splendid tackling robbed Runcorn of at least two tries. Garritty and Bate if not so brilliant, still displayed good defensive powers. The two rival backs, Boscow and Houghton,were very much criticised by the crowd. If Houghton did something out of the ordinary, the Runcornians would ask: the Warringtonians whether Boscow could do that, while the Warringtonians would erow over the Runcornians if Boscow did well. Nothing pleased the spectator better than to see one or the other mull the ball, and this happened more than usual that afternoon owing to the dazzling sun. The Runcorn team was a very well balanced lot, and each department worked well. Warder was probably the most prominent man next to Houghton in it.

In the losing match with St. Helens Recs., on Saturday Warrington were very much weakened,an that the result is not very surprising. The Recs. ars a great stumbling block to Warrington, and indeed both the St. Helens clubs are, for during the present season Warrington have only scored one try against them, whilst there had been no fewer than 28 points scored by the two St. Helens clubs. Three of the matches have been lost, and the remaining one drawn. This does not compare well with last year,for there were then five matches played with the two St. Helens clubs, three were won, one drAwn, and one lost.

Warrington scored a win on Monday, defeating Lan- caster by the narrow majority of one try. This avenged a defeat which Warrington sustained on November 3rd. The play on Monday was not very exciting, but it is satisfactory that a win was obtained. Warrington almost invariably win against Lancaster at Warrington, .and lose at Lancaster, so that the result on Monday was in the natural order of things.

Warrington have still three matches to play. The fixture to-day is against their old and dear neighbours from Widnes. Warrington generally win at Warring- ton when opposed by the chemicals, but the latter have been doing so well this season that the result is very doubtful. Still, Warrington were able to make a draw of it at Widnes, and as they can rise to the occasion when necessary,they ought to win at home. Runcorn is tackled on Wednesday night, and this is agai n a diffi- cult task, while the season finishes with narrow, the champions of the second league. This again is a stiff Job. As an incentive to the team to win, it may be mentioned that if these three matches are won, the wins and losses will be just equal. while if they lose,one, they will be on the wrong side. The result up to the present of the matches for Ihe season is 11 wins, 14 losses, and 10 draws. The Warrington men ought to do their utmost to wipe the arrears off.

OHMY'S NEW CIRCUS AT WARRINGTON.

Each night this week,to Mr.Ohnoy's new circus in Queen at., the Warringten people have flocked in crowds, and with the variety of the programme they have not been disappointed. The building itself, considering the won- derfully short space of time in which it was erected, is very spacious, airy, and well lighted, while the arrange- ments are Plurh that every person has a complete view of the arena,while cemfortable seating is also not lost sight of. Each week the programme is to be changed,so that the source of attraetion will be constantly renewed. Commencing at 7.30 on Monday evening the band under the conductorship of Captain Metcalf, entered upon the " bill of fare " with a sOrited overture. Following thia. Mies Annetti,the great American lady rider,was ushered into the ring, and before she left, she had earned for herself considerable applause by the daring and accomp- lished feats of horsemanship she displayed. While the riding was going on, "The Funny Six" (clowns) pro- vided humorous attractions by their nimble tnmbling:A tight wire ha ring been rapidly erected the sisters Levon proceeded to give an exceedingly clever performance, consisting of balancing, tight wire walking, and various other feats, all of which were performed with grace and skill by these talented young ladies. Two handsome brown penies, lei by Mr. Ohmy in person, gave evidence of cart ful training in the way they " footed it " round the ring, and their successors were the brothers Eltenos who displayed a high type of excel- lence on the horivintsl %ars. One of the two led off with great skill while the other followed with a mimic and laughable attempt to equal his feats. " Napoleon," a beautiful animal,was next "put through his facings" by Miss Allen, who clearly understood her part of the programme, as evidently did the great emperor's name- sake. The next item was rather a startler, not by name but hy natnro. It was announced as the Gate and Bar act on horseb3ck, and the startling part came in with the attire of the rider, who represented " His Infernal Majesty." Nevertheless the rider's " saintly " guise did not prevent him giving an excellent exposition of horse- manshin, and during the same performance Tom Tom proved himself to be a anendid knock about clown. The same clown next had the ring all to himself and made good use of his opportunities by "tumbling " actively through single and triple rings. Mons Zanlo is quite a chair-artist, and he largely interested the spectators by the daring hut confident manner in which he surmounted a pyramid of strong chairs with a pedestal foundation. Claude Ohmy, the son of the genial proprietor, also acquitted himself very creditably in a trick act on horse- back, throughout which, Clown Cartland exercised his wit with amusing effect, after which he was principal in a laughable farce in imitation of the " pomp, pride and panoply" of present day drill. Tremendous applause was accorded the Chicago Trio of Arrobats'and"Tumblern( fafhPr and d a ughters) who proved themselves to he me ryelleus contortionists in addition to possessing consummate tumbling proclivities. By far the most amusing of the varied entertainments given was that afforded by Barnum,the mule that cannot be ridden, and said to be born in Warrington. On the appearance of this singularly mild and innocent looking beast, Mr. , Ohmy offered a prize of 51. to the person who would ride ,it three times round the ring withnut a fall. Several I youths aspired to become possessors of the sum, but that mule knew more than a hit and "chucked "them off quicker than they could get on. Tomato°, the champion jockey rider of the world, was evidently the master of his profesaion and showed wonderful skill in riding, and jumping from the hack of his r•sliciping horse. Whimsi- cal, the Royal Windsor Castle Clown and his wonderful donkey, provid,,d endless amusement, and the donkey especially was decidedly clevir. IS UNIONISM BREAKING UP?

WARNING FROM BIRMINGHAM.

No one will, says yesterday's London " Daily I Chronicle," be surprised at the paragraphs, to which 11 the special and unusual prominence of leaded type is given, that headed the London letter of the " Birmiug- ham Daily Post." The importance of this communica- tion is all the greater when it is remembered that Mr. Robbins, the London correspondent of the " Post," is not only a most reliable and competent journalist,but is in close and constant association with Mr. Chamber- lain. The leader of the Liberal Unionists sees Mr. Robbins frequently, and the " Post," which loyally serves Mr. Chamberlain, is placed by him in steady rapport with his tactics and policy. We have already referred to the anger with which Mr. Chamberlain has received the very unfriendly, not to say brutal, criticism to which he has been subjected in the columns of the "New Review." Mr. Wyndham, a former secretary of Mr. Balfour's, is, we believe, one of the proprietors of that magazine, which is now under air. Henley's editorship. No doubt Mr. Wyndham was in no sense a party to the writing or conception of the article ; but his association with Mr. Balfour makes its appearance rather more unfortunate. In any case Mr. Chamber- lain has thought it worth while to give formal and definite warning to the Conservative party and the Con- servative Press that he is not going to stand this kind of thing much longer. The tone ot the " Standard," of the "St. James's Gazette," and of the " Pall Mall Gazette " has never been friendly, and:two of these papers have throughout deplored Mr. Balfour's action in the matter of the Warwick election. Here are the chief points of the communique which appears in yesterday's " Post."

_ Rumours have been in circulation in the Lobby during the last few days concerning Mr. Chamberlain's position in relation to the recent attacks upon him by the official Conservative Press, and as to the action which he might be expected to take in regard to them. Such tests as I have been able to apply to theso reports lead me to believe that the matter is distinctly of a serious charac- ter. It is said that szrong representation's have been made to the Liberal Unionist whips in reference to the treatment which the right hon. gentleman has received, and that some Members of the party have suggested that a meeting of Liberal Unionists should be held to consider the whole subject. It certainly appears strange that there has not been any public repudiation of the unex- pected and gratuitous accusations of improper motive lodged against Mr. Chamberlain in the columns of the " Standard," a newspaper which is regarded by the public generally as the official organ of the Conservative party. Nobody for a moment believes, and lam indeed in a position to deny, that this ill-natured criticism of Mr. Chamberlain's conduct meets with the approbation of the Conservative leaders, or of any of their supporters in the House of Commons save,perhaps, an unimportant individual here and there. One need hardly point out, however, that it may be difficult to maintain intact a political alliance when jaurnals which are looked upon in the country as the authoi ised exponents of the opinion of one of the parties to the alliance indulge in most un- friendly and unfair criticism of the leader of the other party. I have to-day seen one of the Liberal Unionist candi- dates at the next General Election, who states that the ill-effect produced in the constituency for which he is standing,by the attacks upon Mr. Chamberlain, which a large Dumber of the electors, who have no better know- iedge,cousider to be of authority and importance,is very marked, and that the iocal Gladstonians are disposed to rej )ice over what they regard as the beginning of the end of the Unionist alliance. The opinion which this gentleman expresses may be presumed to be well founded, and it indicates the existence of a strained feeling which is not confined to his constitueucy, but has been created in other quarters. He gives it as his emphatic opinion' in fact, that if the Unionists are to win the next General Election (as, if united, they cer- tainly may be expected to do) these ill-natured and mis- chievious statements respecting Mr. Chamberlain must either cease or be emphatically contradicted on autho- rity as soon as they are put forth. On this, two remarks are to be made. Feeling against Mr. Chamberlain is not confined to the Conservative Press. It exists widely on the Tory side in the House of Commons, and it is a common remark on the part of Mr. Balfour's followers that the sooner the Unionist alliance is ended the better. This feeling is not due solely or perhaps largely to personal dislike of Mr. Chamberlain, though this consideration exists. Strong party Tories feel that the Liberal Unionists, who repre- sent no electoral force in the country, occupy seats to which they have no right, and that they tend to keep the Tory party in a permanent minority in the State. What these gentlemen want is the absolute independence of Toryism in matters of policy, and an opportunity of returning its full strength to the House of Commons. This is the point where the Unionist alliance seems to interpose a permanent obstacle. The other point is still more vital. There are grow- ing differences of policy between Mr. Balfour, and Mr. Chamberlain wants an electoral programme,Mr. Balfour does not. The Tory leader unquestionably thinks that a promise of quiet administration, backed by strong sup- port of the Church, is quite sufficient to bring the Tories back to power. A very powerful wave of opinion on the old Churchy lines in passing over the Tary ranks,and thi, new sentiment Mr. Chamberlain deeply offended by Ii v te on Welsh Disestablishment. On the other ha, di, tte Liberal Unionists are getting alarmed at the Tory leanings towards Fair Trade. Apropos of Mr. Nelson's Protectionist tendencies, the " Birmingham Post" the other day declared that "if Free Trade and free corn are to be put in the balance against an Irish Nationalist Parliament, then we say that thelatter would be an infinitely lesser evil than a return to the system of Protection."_

All these things may not mean an immediate rupture. But they bode a state of things which cannot last. Mr. Chamberlain am his side has by no means, we believe, decided that he will finally cast in his lot with Toryism. And the Tries have certainly not made up their minds that they want Mr. Chamberlain for ever and ever.

GOOD FRIDAY AT WARRINGTON.

BOLD STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH MISSIONARY ANNIVERSARY.

The annual missionary meeting of the Bold-street Wesleyan Church was held on Friday night. The chair was occupied by Dr. T. Starkey Smith, J.P.. and he was supported on the platform by the Revs. J.Kirtlan,Owen Watkins. G. D. Mason, D. W. Johnstone, and E. Strutt, and Mr. W. Bennett. There was a good attendance.

The service was opened by devotional exercises after which the Chairman was introduced to the audience by the Rev. J. Birtian. _ _

The missionary secretary the Rev. G. D. MASON read the annual report, from which it appeared that a very successful year had been passed in all parts of the world. The income of the society for the past year showed an increase of £l,OOO upon the present year. The total was £122,211 18s. 61 and the expenditure £129,517 113 i. 11d., and there was thus a deficiency upon the year of £7,305 18s. 5d., leaving a total deficiency upon the society of £28,827 17s. 4d. The income of Warrington circuit for the past year was £248 Os. id, towards which Bold-street Church had contributed £37 17s. 4d., and Bewsey-street Chapel, £l7 3s. 2d. The juvenile association had contributed a very satisfactory amount towards that sum.

The Rev. E. STRIITT, of Ceylon, then addressed the meeting. Ref rring to objections taken to the mission- ary policy that too many Europeans and too few native preachers were used in the mission field, he said that perhaps in some of the newer fields there might be a large proportion of Europeans, because the native preachers had to be taught, but in the older places, as Ceylon, for instance, that objection would not hold, as there was a very large proportion of native agents, in fact he could fill his speech with an account of those native preachers. He thought that the glory of the Methodist Church was the great army of unpaid preachers. He wished to address himself upon the question, as to whether that was the case with the Methodism abroad as it was at home. He contended that it did, and gave instances to prDve this assertion. One of the most remarkable developments in Ceylon was the work of the women. The women in that country had to surpass numerous barriers, but notwith- standing this, they were doing glorious work. The Rev. D. W. JOHNSTONE said that some years ago Warrington paid him alhigh compliment—that was otin- viting him to be thoir circuit minister,but circumstances over which they had no control prevented him from coming. He publicly thanked them that night for that invitation. He thought the objet of the meeting was a noble one, for what could be nobler than the evangelisa- tion and uplifting of the human race. It would be a sad day for this country if she overlooked her obligation to the heathen. He was glad however that that wo-uld not be so for the present at any .rate, as he boasted and gloried in the fact of being in the land of liberty. The progress of this country and all its greatness was, he contended,in accordance with the spraed of Christianity. Human beings everywhere were religious beings. It was as natural for a man to worship as it was to breathe. He therefore pleaded that they take the glorious gospel to the hesthen, and let them know the greatness of it as they did themselves. The cause of Christianity was continually spreading all over the world, he asked those wholwere present to contribute something to further on the cause. Thousands upon thousands were waiting for this christanity, and he called upon them to provi.le. the society with funds to be able to send men to make known the grandest movement upon the earth which was the salvation of the world. A collection was then taken in aid of the Foreign missions. _ _

The Rev. Owen Watkins afterwards addressed the meeting. At the close of the nicotine hearty votes of thanks were accorded the chairman and deputation of their pre- sence that evening, and the meeting waa brought to a conclusion by the proaouncing of the benediction.

MODERATE SOCIALISM.

Sir,-1 should like to have some of Mr. Brouchetnite's views and reasons in regard to Socialism. I believe that the Lords deserve no sympathy because they would not even censent to self reform, yet it is only by complete obstructiou that they may he liable to complete ex-,inc- tion. We cannot unconditionally get rid ot the obliga- tions imposed on us wrongly or rightly by our forefathers in regard to them, but there is no commonsense reason why the titles and the pensions should not be conferred after the death of their present holders and their heirs upon some of the more eserving of the coming genera- tionsrend in that way matte thein rewards for services render-ed. . Whatever reasons there may be for doing away with Royalty, when compared with France and America, we may not forget Napoleon's extravagance, that America is yet a growing nation, and that in Royalty we have a Queen who, at least, is a lesson to matrons of this land for motherly sympathy. In .Royalty have we, if used rightly and impartially, a guarantee against any se•ltion that may possibly grow over-bearingly factious?
