A READER.

A SCHOOL BOARD FOR WARRINGTON.

Sir,—l trust.none of yours readers were so misguided as to respond to the begging letter which you printed last Saturday on behalf of St. Peter's Schools. I cannot conceive why people should be so solicitous to obtain means from private sources to do work which should be done by the ratepayers generally. The education of the young of our town is a work, the responsibility of which rightly rests upon the whole of burgesses, and so long as the ratepayers permit private people to educate our children so long do the ratepayers fail to act up to their responsibility. Another phase of the subject is this: By bolstering up these private enterprises we are only making lashes to whip ourselves with when the time comes to take over the schools. Why do not people see the folly of supporting sectarian schools,and goin boldly for a School Board, one of the greatest needs of War- rington at the present hcur. We should then have done with these pathetic appeals for funds, and the work of education would be infinitely better done.—Yours &c., A PROGRESSIVE.

===== 0002601_18950420_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
SOCIALISM.

Sir,—l had thought to have taken a recess from Socia- listic considerations, but I see they have got the cork out again, and again there is much spume and lather lying about. So I propese once more to " blow the top off "to see if there is anything more than froth in it. The first bit of spume is from the pen of A. Brouchernite who says "Tony makes statements which I challenge him to prove. He asserts that eocialietio colonies have been started and failed. That is false." So A. 8., who spouts so muck about Socialism is either ignorant of, or knowingly denies the fact el' repeated attempts at coloniz- ation on Socialistic basis. Have I dreamed that some enthusiasts banded themselves together—subscribed £6O each and started in South America—started and failed— a miserable fiasco? Will those who know say if this he true. Is it as I assert, or as A.B. denies ? No ! these en- thusiasts found that they had taken with them one thing—which for the success of their enterprise ought to have been left behind—our common human nature. Then we have "Rosebud " screaming " shame on you Tony I" Surely your correspondent has been unhappy in his;rvegetable nomenclature. From the lugabriously desponding style of this tear-distiller, I should propose, as a name," Weeping Willow," rather than the thornless immature rose. But our friend is immature in argu- mentation as well as in name. I have said that he tries one age by the standard of morality of another. This,poor Rosebud cannot understand. He cannot see that what was considered to be quite right 600 years ago is now considered to be quite wrong. But that our thinking it wrong does not alter the fact of its being thought right then. The law of the strongest was the " right" of that time. Now suppose the Socialists got possession of the land, by what title would they hold it f Precisely by the same title by which it was held by those who wrested it from its old time possessors—the right of might—the fact of their being able to take it and able to hold it. But see the " Rosebud's " confusion of ideas. He wants to pur- suade us that to steal a watch new is equivalent to stealing land then! Now, the question is—Would the robbed if he had had the chance have robbed the rob- ber ? No ; then there is no parallel in the cases. But this is j st the kind of froth by which certain Socialists irst humbug themselves and then try to humbug others. Let them by all means in the future try to keep the cerk in if nothing better than this comes of getting it out. The proposed attack upon the National Debt shows mob socialism in its true light--Robbery.—l am, kc., TONY LUNITKIN.

VESTRY MEETINGS.

LIVELY MEETING AT FRODSEIAM. MR. FOULKES ON THE WARPATH.

The establishment of the church made it a re- R. Bate. markable instance of what he might call socialism ap- plied to Christianity. Some people were never tired of saying that Christianity was essentially socialistic, and he contended that there was no question that the Estab- lished Church was socialistic. It provided a religion for everybody who liked to make use of it free. It used to be at the general expense, now it was at the expense of the endowment of old time. They could not say because a person would not come to church they would not minister to that person. He was provided practically for something that some thought the most important thing in the world. ELECTION OF OFFICERS. The Vicar nominated Mr. J. E. Meredith as his warden for the ensuing year. —Mr. John Littler was appointed people's warden on the motion of Mr. Key, seconded by Mr. John Yates. Crosby 32, G. Hewitt 28, John Yates 25, J. K. Digges 21. The Chairman nominated Mr. J. H. Weston as his sidesman to act as warden at St. Michael's. A. Lightburn, T. Clark and W. Cropper. For Weston Church, Mr. G. Robinson was appointed Vicar's warden; James Ford, jun., people's warden ; and Messrs. W. Mitcheson, John Bankes, E. J. Guest, T. Ford, jun., and Farrell, sidesmen. CHARITIES. • The accounts of the charities were presented by the Chairman. He mentioned that the Rigby charity con- sisted of £3OO which was advanced on mortgage of three freehold houses, one in Wellington-st.,and two in Bridge- water-st., Runcorn. The interest realised £l3 10s. annually, and was expended in providing 55 flannel petticoats. The Alcock charity was £5 16s. 4d.,0f which £3 was paid to the churchwarden and the remainder given away on St. Thomas' Day. The vestry meting was then declared at an end. THE CONGREGATIONAL MEETING. The congregational meeting was then held. The ac- counts showed that:after meeting all expenses at the three churches there were the following balances which were paidto the Vicar for the clergy fund :—Parish Church, £l4O 15s. lid. ; St. Michael's Church, £64 133. 1111. ; St. John's Church, Weston, £2l 53. lid. Littler • St. Michael's, Messrs. W. Mellor and A. Lightburn ; Weston, Messrs. J. Robinson and James Ford, jun. Votes of thanks to the officers and chairman concluded a lengthy meeting. HOLY TRINITY CHURCH, RUNCORN. The annual vestry meeting of this church was held on Tuesday evening. The Rev. Dr. Preston presided. Amongst those present were :---Messrs. C. A. Timmins, W. flankinson, Killcross, Ainsworth. Pearson, Prince, Westbrook, A. Savage, J. H. Salkeld, Furner, Horsefield, Cunninghom, Joseph Pritchard, Woods, Smith and Broughton. The Chairman in the course of a short address referred to the progress which they had made in the parish.—Mr. Charles Timmins in his statement said there was not a debt of any sort owing in con- nection with the church. As would he seen from the balance sheet the receipts had been £1865a. &Land the expenditure £lB6 ss. 6d., leaving a balance in the wardens' hands of 2d. The friendly fund had been ex- pended in a manner which would meet with their ap- proval. There was only one matter that called for words of regret and that was the small amount received for pew rents. The election of officers was then pro- ceeded with, and resulted as follows :—Vicar's warden, Mr. Charles A. Timmins ; people's warden. Mr. Hankin- son ; sidesmen, Messrs. R. Kilcross, G. THE ORGANIST'S SALARY..

Mr. Linaker stated that the organist trustees had ap- pointed Mr. Birkett Musgrove, of Runcorn, organist of the church at £35 per annum. £3O of this amount would be paid from the Organ fund, and the remainder would be made up from the Parish Church fund.

THE FIRST BRREZE.

After the notice convening the meeting had been read Mr. R. Foulkes said he publicly prGtested against that meeting being held in that pace and on that day. It was not hold on that day in other planes and was not held inside a church. They had a good schoolroom and the meeting shnuld hive been held there. Irrespective Of what Mr. Reynolds said last year. the meeting was held at that time and place in order to get a certain clique together. Free speech was practically boycotted in a place like that. "IS THERE PERFECT PEACE AND HARMONY ?"

Mr. Linaker read the minutes of the last meeting which were cirefully followed and checked by Mr. Foulkes. At the close Mr. Foulkes said that a sentence of the Vicar's had been left out—viz. : " That the Vicar closed a discussion, saying that they had perfect peace and harmony in the parish."—Mr.Linaker. said thy did

CHARITIES.

The accounts of the Frodsham and Stockham charity, Harrison charity,and the Upton charity,were submitted. —Mr. Foulkes asked whether the charities were strictly confined to people going to that church or were they left to the parishioners generally.—Mr. Linaker said one charity was strictly confined to those who attended tbat church, but it had not been strictly observed.—The Chairman said that with regard to the Harrison's charity that was distributed quite impartially.—Mr. Lewis said they were mostly dissenters who had the benefit of that charity.

THANKS. The Chairntan proposed that the thanks of that vestry be given to the adult singers at both churches. He alluded to the self-sacrificing way the singers had ful- filled their duties.—Mr. Foulkes seconded, and said he would feel greatly obliged if the Vicar and singers would not chant the responses to the ten commandments. He thought it was an outrage to petition the Lord with musical accompaniments.—Mr. Heywood proposed a vote of thanks to the ladies for their services in decorat- ing the church on various occasions with flowers.—Mr. C. E. Linaker seconded.—Mr. Foulkes protested against the ladies bringing flowers into a place of worship. They were not required. The flowers might make up for de- ficiencies in the service, but they ought not to be used at all in a place of worship. The motion was carried.

ANOTHER PROTEST BY MR. FOULKES.

-- The Chairman asked if any parishoner desired to intro- duce any subject. Mr. Foulkes said he entered his protest against their , having introduced into the church the last edition of ' hymns Ancient and Modern. Ile hymn 622 dedicated to the Virgin Mary was sufficient In itself to condemn the book. It was rank worship to the creature, defying the Creator, and was anti-Christian. He rejected and protested against the doctrine commonly known as 1" Baptismal regeneration." that was, that all infants were regenerated at baptism, as a pernicious Romish error. It was taught in hymn 326. They knew that infant baptism could not be found in the New Testa- ment. He protested against the congregation being asked to sing hymns 105, 197 as was done in the Iron Church, Frodsham, on the 7th inst. and Sunday in that church. The Romanizers inside the Church of England paid worship to a literal cross, and told the people a cross was to be erected over the grave of the departed in order to secure repose. The cross was a superstitious, idolatrous and hea- then emblem. Who ever read a more Pop- ish hymn than the 96th ? He protested against pro- fessional and recessional hymns. They were a direct violation of the Rubric, which stated that at the begin- ning of morning prayer the minister should read with a large voice some one or more of these sentences of the scriptures. It was done to favour Parsonolatry or paying homage to clergy and choir when entering the church— in other words man-worship. Sacerdotalism grew nat- urally out of ambassador-worshin. It was an endeavour to " lord it over God's heritage." He protested against the clergy and choir turning to the East. It was a violation of the Rubric, preceding the Nicene creed. It was a relic of paganism or sun-worship. As the Iron Church at Frodsham was built North and South it could not be the Eastward position, but teaching the people to believe that the bcdy of Christ bad so recently lain on the so-called altar. What did the curate pray to when kneel- ing before a brass cross over the Communion Table ? Was not that rank idolatry—a breaking of the second Commandment? He entered his protest against their narrowness in strictly adhering to the first Rubric in the burial service, in not saying the service over an uncon- scious infant, w ho had not had a drop of water sprinkled on its face. It was with great difficulty that Mr.Foulkes pro ceeded thus far, and be was repeatedly interrupted and several present left the meeting.—The Chairman and others strongly objected to Mr. Foulkes proceeding.—Mr. Foulkes tried again to proceed with his prepared address but the Chairman said he must call him to order. He must have a resolution or close the meeting .—Mr. Foulkes then moved that Mr. Bloag or the clergy of the church should be guided by the Rubric in the Book of Common Prayer, having assented and consented to the 39 articles of the Church of England, to the Book of Homilies and to the canons of the church. —The Chair- man objected to put such a resolution. Flo was only answerable for his conduct to the Bishop and not to that vestry. If he did anything contrary to the laws of the Church nt England he would have to take the responsi- bility.—Mr. Foulkes : That is a nice way of getting out of it. He was proceeding to speak when Mr. Reynolds said if .Mr. Foulkes was in a minority of one they should decline to listen to him further.—The Chairman asked if anyone was prepared to second the resolution. Receiv- ing no reply,he said he would declare the meeting at an end.—Mr. Riley proposed a vote of thanks to the Chair- man, churchwardens, and sidesmen.—Mr. Edwards se- conded, and the motion was carried.

St JOHN'S, EARLESTOWN.

. The annual vestry meeting was held in the church es Mouthy afternoon. The Rev. T. R. Matthews presided, and amongst others prevent were the Rev.E.W.W. Kayo, Messrs. T. A. Pimblett, P. Foden, H. H. Anderton, J. Jones,&c. Mr. Pimblett,warden, presented the accounts for the year, showing an adverse balance of 3985 ss.4id. The Vicar nominated Mr. Pimblett as his warden, and on the motion of Mr. Winstanley, seconded by Mr. J. Latham, Mr. Foden was re-appointed parishioner's warden. The whole of the retiring sidesmen were re- appointed. A voluntary church rate of twopence in the pound was laid. The statement of accounts of the Dean Trust Cbarity,were presented, showing that £47 I3s. 9d. had been spent on the four exhibitions, and inciden- tal expenses. The churchwardens were elected repre- sentatives to the Diocesan Conferences. The Vicar thanked the wardens and sidesm.n for their services during the year. They had, he said, worked very hard and harmoniously. He also tendered his thanks to the choir for their arduous duties. A vote of thanks was accorded Mrs. Latimer, the organist, on the motion of Mr. Winstanley seconded by blir. Latham. Mr. J.Jones suggested that'a contribution should be made each year to some Convalescent Home,in order that they might be in a better position to send poor parishioners thore,when recovering from an illness. After considerable discus- sion, it was decided on the suggestion of the 'Vicar that the collections or one Sunday in the year should be divided between an Eye Hospital, Infirmary, and Con- valescent Home. A vote of thanks to the Vicar termin- ated the proceedings. SF. PETER'S, NEWTON.

_ The annual vestry meeting of the parish of St. Peter's, Newton, was held in the Town Hall, Newton, on Tues• day evening. The Rev. H. Monk presided, and there were present Dr. Watkins, Messrs. W. H. Hindley, J. Liptrott, I. Bateman,J. Clarke, L. Wilkinson, W. Speak- man, J. H. Moicrop, T. Ritchie, Col. Wood, &c. The statement of accounts was presented by Mr. J. Liptrott, vicar's warden. who gave receipts as £94 23. 5d., and expenditure £93 Os.sd.,leaving a balanceof £1 2s.in fav- our of the wardens. He said, if he did not say something with regard to the working of the year, he should be neglectful of his duty. Last year there was a balance arising from their accounts of £1 Os. Bd., which was a balance left to them by their predecessors. Shortly after taking office they received a demand from the Insurance Company for payment of the premiums, and instead of having a balance of £1 03. 6d. in their favour, it became a balance of £2 9s. Od. against them. He did not men- tion this as being any reflection upon their predecessors, but thay had £2 93. to find, and practically had to ;pay the insurance twice in one year.—Mr. Hindley said he had nothing to do with that, as the additional insurance was passed at the vestry meeting Mr. Liptrott said it was no reflection upon the previous wardens, but it was unfortunate that the new wardens should be in that position. He then alluded to the reduction of the church expenses, pointing out that they had been compelled to reduce the sexton's salary to £4O per year. At the end of the year they found they would be something like £2O to the bad, aad they made an appeal to the congregation, and be had to thank Colonel McCorquodale for his handsome donation of 10 guineas, which with the other general subscriptions gave them a snsall balance. He would like the members of the con- gregation to put it to themselves and say the child' needs £lOO per year to carry it on, and each person should undertake to give a certain sum per year to the expenses of the church. He mentioned several things which required doing, but could not be carried out owing to wat.t of funds.—Dr. Watkins proposed that the warden's accounts be received and passed subject to them being found correct, and in order. He congratulated the war- dens upon thelr success in their somewhat difficult year ot office ; what he would advice the wardens to do when they get their accounts printed, was that they set forth clearly to the congregation what would be the probable expenses of the year.— Colonel Wood seconded, and the accounts were adopted. —The statement of accounts of the Dean School Charity were presented.—The Rev. H. Monk testified to the very great value of the services of the church officers during the past year. It was a considerable number of years since they had had such a pleasant and comfort- able year. He renominated 85r.Liptrott as his warden.— Mr. I. Bateman was re-appointed parishioners' warden. —Dr. Fl. Watkins, Messrs. W. Speakman, A. Smith, and J. H.Lane were nominated Vicar's sidesmen,Messrs. , Percival, Meyer, Ritchie, and Bly thing were appointed people's sidesmen.—The wardens were appointed repre- sentatives of the congregation at the Ruri-deaconal Con- ferences.—Dr. Watkins proposed, and Mr. T. Wilkinson seconded a vote of thanks to the wardens and sidesmen, and it was adopted.—A vote of thanks to the Vicar as Chair man closed the proceedings.

WARRINGTON PARISH CHURCH.

The annual vestry meeting in connection with the above church was held at the National Schoolroom on Tuesday evening. There was a good attendance, and the chair was occupied by the Rector (Rev. F. W. Willis). and there were also present the Revs. A. A.Markham and H. M. R. Hull, Councillor R. T. Fairclough (Rector's warden), Councillor A. Woods (people's warden). STATEMENT OF ACCOUNTS. Councillor Woods in giving the annual statement of accounts said that he would first express his pleasure that the Rector was present that evening after his illness and he hoped that he would COMP back from the little holiday he was about to take, fit for his duties. In pre- ' 'elating the accounts, he was glad that they had at last got to the bottom of the mysterious accounts which had been floating about for several years. Be hoped to put before them something definite, and he did not think they would be troubled any more with any old accounts cropping up, Considering the fearful state of trade dur- ing the twl previous years he was glad to say that the receipts amounted to £564 lie. 7d., but he must tell them that the expenditure amounted to £826 13s. 10d. He was sure that they would admit that if the church was going to pieces, it would be better at once to patch it up, and make a good permanent job, than to anneal to the people in a few years to contribute £2,000 or £3,000 to practically restore it. At the present time, the Parish Church inside and out with the exception of a little stonework, was in perfect order. He wished to impress upon the people the necessity of giv- ing more to the offertory. He thought they should at least give a penny in the morning and another in the afternoon. If that were done, they would easily raise £BOO or £l,lOO a year.—Mr. S. Davies moved, and Mr. Walton seconded the adoption of the minutes, which were passed unanimously. T OF WARDENS. The Rector anno•mced that he had prevailed upon Mr. R. T. Fairclough to be his warden for the ensuing year. Mr. Price moved, and Mr. Banner seconded the elec- tion of Mr. A. Woods as people's warden. The motion was carried unanimously.

ELECTION OF SIDESIIEN. Mr. R. T. Fairclough nominated Mr. John Welton.and Mr. Woods, Mr. Arthur Browne, as their respective sidesmen for the ensuing year. The election of eight sidesmen was then procceeded with. The following gentlemen were nominated : Messrs. Gill, Price, Craik, Clark, Mason, DaviesMoores, Dewhurst, Wright, Scotson, and Atkin. A vote was then taken with the result that Messrs. Gill, Price, Craik, Mason, Davies, Moores, Dewhurst, and Wright were elected,

Mr. Bviner was elected sidesman for St. George's Mission, and Mr, F. J. Whittle for Bank-st. Mission. LAY DELEGATES.

THE EASTER OFFERIEGS.

Mr. Chantry proposed that the offertories on Easter Sunday be given to the 'Vicar. Mr. Finney seconded the motion. Mr. A. J. Jolley said it had been usual to reserve the Easter offerings for the Sunday school prize fund, and he would certainly vote against any change be'rig made in the matter. He had been connected with St. Paul's Church for a great number of years, and, as they knew, took great interest in the work of the garish, , and he thought it would be seen that he objected merely on principle to the Easter offerings being devoted to other than the Sunday school prize fund. After some discussion, in which the Vicar said he must decline to receive the Easter offerings if any objection was raised, Mr. Chantry withdrew his motion, ond the Easter offer- ings will for the present at all events be devoted to the Sunday school prize fund. PROPOSED MEMORIAL TO THE LATE MRS. JOHN CMORLEY. The vicar said the church on the previous day had re- ceived a present in the shape of a handsome book con- taining the Communion office. It was the gift of the members of the " mothers' meeting," with which the late Mrs. John Chorley had manifested consider- able interest for many years. The inscrip- tion on the book read : •‘ This book was given to St. Paul's Church in memory of Mrs. John Chorley by the members of the mothers' meeting, Easter Day, 1895." The Vicar thought that something might be done by the congregation and the parishioners gener- ally in memory of Mrs. Chorley, whose work in connec- tion with the church and parish had beea so valuable. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. Chantry suggested that the repair of the tower clock or the erection of a new clock in its place would be a fitting memorial to the deceased lady. —Mr. Jeans said he was not a parishioner, but he had an interest in the parish, and he suggested that the more appropriate way in which to show their respect for Mrs. Chorley was to erect a tablet in the church.—The Chair- man thought the two suggestions might be combined, but it was not necessary to submit resolutions that day.

PROPOSED RENOVATION OF THE CHURCH.

The Rev. E. C. E. Carleton proposed that the vestry should set aside £lO annually out of the collections to form a repairing fund with it. By so doing they would have money to fall back upon to renovate the church when it was necessary, instead of having to collect sub- scriptions. The motion was carried.—lt was intimated that renovations and improvements would shortly be effected in the interior of the church.

ST. JAMES'S, LATCHEORD.

The annual Easter vestry meeting of the above church was held on Monday at noon. The Rev. F. Slater pre- sided, and there were also present—The Rev. E. T. Hull, and Messrs. C. S. Burgess, J. H. Warburton, T. Hutch- inson, W. Eckersley, J. Caldwell, E. Archbald, T. Jack- son, and J. Dobson..—Mr. C. S. Burgess submitted the churchwardens' account for the past year, showing that the offertories yielded a total of £Bl 14s. id. The ex- penditure showed that there had been a balance due to ' the wardens at Easter, 1894, amounting to £l5 Bs. 3-0. The special expenses amounted to £lOO 16s. no. Other items brought the total disbursements up to £240 15s, 6d. Continuing, Mr. Burgess remarked that they had now completed the series d six pictures in thq church, and he did not doubt that every member of the Church would agree with him in saying that they added very much to the appearance of the walls. They had also got a new pulpit that year, which had been given to them by the Rector and wardens of Grappenhall. This was also a welcome addition to the church, increasing as it did the comfort of the preachers. There was something, how- ever, which he had had constantly to mention at these annual vestry meetings, and that was the large amount of money which they bad to receive from outside sources towards the general expenses ot the church. It must be a matter of regret to those who worshipped in that church that Ihe offertories, instead ot increasing, were really diminishinz. When he looked back upon the time during which he had been a churchwarden he could not help noticing that the offertories had consi- derably decreased from year to year. This was, of course, a matter entirely for those who attended the church to consider; but he himself Could not but think that it would be far more satisfactory if the offertories were increasing than decreasing, and that those who were members would try to do more than they really were doing for the church. (Hear, hear.)—The accounts were passed, on the proposition of Mr. Hutchinson, seconded by Mr Eckertiley.

===== 0002601_18950420_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
APPOINTMENT OF WARDENS AND SIDESMEN,

I The Vicar said he could not tell how glad he was that Mr. Burgess had again consented to be nominated as his (the Vicar's) warden. Mr. Burgess was always protest- ing that he had held the position long enough, but his (the Vicar's) opinion was that when they once had a good man they must keep him. (Hear, hear.) Mr.Burgess,in accepting the office for the ninth time, thanked the Vicar for this renewal of confidence. He must, however, express his regret that the Vicar had not selected another person to fill the place. He could not help thinking that a change in blood would have wrought more benefit in the church and parish, such as

the introduction of new ideas and the infusion of fresh / energy were likely to produce. He took the post some- what reluctantly in some respects,though not in others; but he hoped that at the next vestry meeting they would select someone with more energy and ability to occupy the office.—Mr. J. 1:i. Warburton was re-elected people's wardee, on the motion of Mr. Eckersley. se- conded by Mr. Jackson.—Messrs. George Venn,T. Jack- son J. Shaw, B. Cotton,J. Millwood, and H. Stansfield, who were the sidesmen for the previous year, were re- elected, with the addition of Mr. W. Eckersley, for the ensuing twelve months.—Messrs. C. H. Burgess and H. N. Houghton were re-appointed lay delegates.

VOTES OF THANKS.

Mr. Hutchinson moved a vote of thanks to Messrs. C. S. Burgess and J. H. Warburton for their valuable ser- vices as wardens of the church, He said that both gentlemen were heartily deserving of such a compliment and he was sure that the vestry were well pleased that both had again accepted office.—Mr. Jackson seconded, and the vote having been unanimously accorded, Mr. Burgess brietly returned thanks.

===== 0002601_18950420_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
CONTEMPLATED STRUCTURAL ALTERATIONS.

The Vicar thought there was no need to transact further business at that meeting, seeing that they would have to meet again very soon. A turret clock had been bequeathed to the church by the late Mr. Charles Middleton, and also some money by the same deceased gentleman for the purpose of purchasing a stained glass window and placing it in the church to the memory of his deceased father and mother. It would be necessary to obtain a faculty for this work to be carried out, and at the same time they proposed to ask for powers to effect certain alterations in the church, the advisability of which had already been freely discussed. It would, however, be requisite to have another parish meeting in a few weeks' time or so.

The proceedings closed with a vote of thanks to the Vicar for presiding.

Mr. James had written to him to say that his health was so affected that his doctor had ad- vised him to leave the country permanently, and he had therefore,decided to leave for America,where his parents lived. He (the speaker) was sure that there was no one present who would not very much regret that they were about to lose such valuable services.—Mr. Lee seconded the motion, which was carried, some discussion taking place upon the advisability of dividing the duties now held by Mr. James.—Mr. E. THE INDEPENDENT LABOUR PARTY...

The annual Conference of the Independent Labour• party was opened on Monday at Ne.wcastle-on- ryne- The annual report of the Administrative Council,whichl was presented by Mr. T. Mann, recommended amongss, other things the printing of an" international circular,' in English, French, and German, dealing with Socialism in this country, and sending greetings to the Socialists ot all countries. A close alliance with the co-operative' movement was advised, "as co-operation is at the bottom democratic, and undoubtedly makes for the common organisation of industry in the interests workers." The report was adopted. In the course t the proceedings it was resolved to retain the name SPY which the party has hitherto been known. Its aim was, defined to be the establishment of "an Industrie of tbe commonwealth founded uoon the socialisation land and capital," and this was to be effected "by the education of the community in the principles of Social ism, the 'industrial and political organisation of t!le:.. workers, and the independent representation of Sociarisk• principles on all elective bodies." The Conference of the Independent Labour party was resumed at Newcastle on Tuesday, when the Presl- dent (Mr. Hardie, M.P.) delivered his inaugural address• He claimed for the organisation that it had every ar pearance of s'ability and success. The Liberal party,ne, declared, was "shedding its members at both He believed the time was opportune for the (canatie, of a Socialistic party which should have as its cleclar.en. aim "an industrial commonwealth," in which the In struments for the production of wealth should be owned la in common, and in which the wealth produced be used for the advantage of the entire coarmllo4;0 Consideration was resumed of the proposals of National Administrative Council, and resolutions were passed on a considerable variety of subjects.
